fulfill the condition (3.16) (the high entropy limit being necessary to ensure that the associated quantum Gaussian states behave as classical Gaussian probability distributions).
Let us hence suppose to apply a transformation on the input modes of the system which depends on a real parameter t that plays the role of an effective temporal coordinate, and which is constructed in such a way that, starting from t=0 from the input state \hat{\rho}_{X} it will drive the modes towards such optimal Gaussian configurations in the asymptotic limit t\rightarrow\infty — see Section 3.3.3.
Accordingly for each t\geq 0 we will have an associated value for the entropies S_{\alpha} and S_{Y} which, if the qEPI is correct, should still fulfill the bound (3.33).
To verify this it is useful to put the qEPI (3.33) in the rate form
\frac{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\lambda_{\alpha}\;\exp[{S_{\alpha}}/{n}]}{\exp[{S_{Y}}/{n}]}\leq 1\;.
(3.37)
We will then study the left-hand-side of Eq. (3.37) showing that its
parametric derivative is always positive (see Section 3.3.8) and that that for t\rightarrow\infty it tends to 1 (see Section 3.3.9).
3.3.1 The Liouvillian
The parametric evolution suitable for the proof will be given in terms of a quantum generalization of the classical Laplacian, that we define in this Section.
Let \gamma\geq 0 be a positive semi-definite real matrix.
We define the Liouvillian
\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}\left(\hat{X}\right):=\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{ij}\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{X}\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}\right|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}}\;,
(3.38)
where the sum over the repeated indices is implicit.
\mathcal{L}_{\gamma} is linear in \gamma , commutes with hermitian conjugation:
\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}\left(\hat{X}^{\dagger}\right)=\left(\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}\left(\hat{X}\right)\right)^{\dagger}\;,
(3.39)
and is self-adjoint with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt product:
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}\left(\hat{X}\right)\;\hat{Y}\right)=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{X}\;\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}\left(\hat{Y}\right)\right)\;.
(3.40)
Taking the characteristic function of both sides of (3.38), and recalling Eq. (A.20) of Appendix A, we get
\chi_{\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}\left(\hat{X}\right)}(\mathbf{k})=-\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,\gamma\,\mathbf{k}^{T}\;\chi_{\hat{X}}(\mathbf{k})\;.
(3.41)
If we formally define the exponential of \mathcal{L}_{\gamma} , Eq. (3.41) can be easily integrated into
\chi_{e^{\mathcal{L}(\gamma)}\left(\hat{X}\right)}(\mathbf{k})=e^{-\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,\gamma\,\mathbf{k}^{T}}\;\chi_{\hat{X}}(\mathbf{k})\;,
(3.42)
and e^{\mathcal{L}(\gamma)} can be easily recognized as the additive-noise channel that can be recovered from (A.54) with M=\mathbb{I} , \alpha=\gamma and \mathbf{y}=\mathbf{0} .
This channel adds to the state noise with covariance matrix \gamma , acts on the moments as
\displaystyle\sigma
\displaystyle\mapsto
\displaystyle\sigma+\gamma
(3.43)
\displaystyle\mathbf{r}
\displaystyle\mapsto
\displaystyle\mathbf{r}\;,
(3.44)
and hence on the Gaussian state \hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma,\,\mathbf{x}) as
e^{\mathcal{L}(\gamma)}\left(\hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma,\,\mathbf{x})\right)=\hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma+\gamma,\,\mathbf{x})\;.
(3.45)
3.3.2 Useful properties
In the proof, we will need some properties of the channel defined in (3.20) and of the Liouvillian (3.38).
From (3.20) and (A.20) the action of \Phi on translations follows:
\Phi\left(\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{X}\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}\right)=\hat{D}(M\mathbf{x})\;\Phi\left(\hat{X}\right)\;{\hat{D}(M\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}\;.
(3.46)
We can now use (3.46) and (3.38) to compute the action of \Phi on \mathcal{L}_{\gamma} :
\Phi\left(\mathcal{L}(\gamma)\left(\hat{X}\right)\right)=\mathcal{L}\left(M\gamma M^{T}\right)\left(\Phi\left(\hat{X}\right)\right)\;,
(3.47)
and hence
\Phi\left(e^{\mathcal{L}(\gamma)}\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\right)=e^{\mathcal{L}\left(M\gamma M^{T}\right)}\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\right)\;.
(3.48)
3.3.3 The evolution
The idea of the proof is to evolve the inputs (and consequently the output) toward Gaussian states with very high entropies and with covariance matrices satisfying (3.16).
For this purpose, we use the additive-noise channel that we have just defined in (3.42).
Let us fix a positive matrix \gamma , and define for each \alpha
\gamma_{\alpha}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda_{\alpha}\;M_{\alpha}^{-1}\;\gamma\;M_{\alpha}^{-T}&\text{if}\;\lambda_{\alpha}>0\\
0&\text{if}\;\lambda_{\alpha}=0\\
\end{array}\right.\;,
(3.49)
such that
M_{\alpha}\;\gamma_{\alpha}\;M_{\alpha}^{T}=\lambda_{\alpha}\;\gamma\;.
(3.50)
Let t be the time of the evolution.
We apply to the \alpha -th input the additive-noise channel e^{t_{\alpha}(t)\;\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{\alpha})} , with a time-dependent coefficient t_{\alpha}(t) to be determined:
\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}(t):=e^{t_{\alpha}(t)\;\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{\alpha})}(\hat{\rho}_{\alpha})\;.
(3.51)
We notice that, if some \lambda_{\alpha}=0 , we are not evolving at all the corresponding state \hat{\rho}_{\alpha} .
From (3.48) and (3.49), the evolution (3.51) of the input mode induces the temporal
evolution of the output modes
\Phi\left(\left(\bigotimes_{\alpha=1}^{K}e^{t_{\alpha}(t)\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{\alpha})}\right)\hat{\rho}\right)=e^{t_{Y}(t)\mathcal{L}\left(\gamma\right)}\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\right)\;,
(3.52)
where
t_{Y}(t)=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\lambda_{\alpha}t_{\alpha}(t)\;.
(3.53)
From (3.42), the characteristic functions evolve as
\chi_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})(t)=e^{-\frac{1}{4}t_{\alpha}(t)\,\mathbf{k}\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,\mathbf{k}^{T}}\;\chi_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})(0)\;,
(3.54)
so that if \lambda_{\alpha}>0 and t_{\alpha}(t)\to\infty for t\to\infty , the evolved state \hat{\rho}_{\alpha}(t) is asymptotic to the Gaussian state \hat{\rho}_{G}\left(t_{\alpha}(t)\,\gamma_{\alpha}\right) , that satisfies (3.16) with c_{\alpha}(t)=\lambda_{\alpha}t_{\alpha}(t) and \sigma=\gamma for any choice of t_{\alpha}(t) .
However, for initial Gaussian states that almost saturate the EPI, i.e.
\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}(0)=\hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma_{\alpha})
(3.55)
with the \sigma_{\alpha} having large symplectic eigenvalues and satisfying (3.16), the evolved \sigma_{\alpha}(t) must still almost saturate the EPI and then satisfy (3.16) also for finite t , i.e. the time-evolved version of the A_{\alpha}
A_{\alpha}(t):=M_{\alpha}\;\sigma_{\alpha}(t)\;M_{\alpha}^{T}=c_{\alpha}\;\sigma+\lambda_{\alpha}\;t_{\alpha}(t)\;\gamma
(3.56)
must remain proportional (we have used (3.45) to get the time evolution).
For this purpose, we use the freedom in the choice of t_{\alpha}(t) , defining them as the solutions of
\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}t_{\alpha}(t)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\mu_{\alpha}(t)
\displaystyle t_{\alpha}(0)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle 0\;,
(3.57)
where we have defined
\mu_{\alpha}(t):=e^{S\left(\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}(t)\right)/n}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{n}S\left(e^{t_{\alpha}(t)\;\mathcal{L}\left(\gamma_{\alpha}\right)}\left(\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right)\;.
(3.58)
This is a first-order differential equation for the functions t_{\alpha}(t) , and under reasonable assumptions on the regularity of the function
t_{\alpha}\mapsto S\left(e^{t_{\alpha}\;\mathcal{L}\left(\gamma_{\alpha}\right)}\left(\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}\right)\right)
(3.59)
always admits a unique solution.
Let us check that the evolution defined by (3.57) has the required properties.
First, since quantum entropies are nonnegative we have
\frac{d}{dt}t_{\alpha}(t)\geq 1\;,
(3.60)
so that
\lim_{t\to\infty}t_{\alpha}(t)=\infty\;.
(3.61)
The differential equation (3.57) allows us to define equivalently the A_{\alpha}(t) as the solutions of
\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}A_{\alpha}(t)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha}\;\mu_{\alpha}(t)\;\gamma
\displaystyle A_{\alpha}(0)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle c_{\alpha}\;\sigma\;,
(3.62)
where we have used (3.56).
Using (A.41) to approximate the entropy of a Gaussian state with a large covariance matrix, the coefficients \mu_{\alpha}(t) are given by
\mu_{\alpha}(t)\simeq\frac{e}{2}\left(\det\sigma_{\alpha}(t)\right)^{\frac{1}{2n}}\;.
(3.63)
Let us put into (3.62) the ansatz of proportional A_{\alpha}(t) :
A_{\alpha}(t):=c_{\alpha}\;\sigma(t)\;.
(3.64)
Then, the system of K differential equations in (3.62) reduces to only one equation for \sigma(t) :
\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\sigma(t)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\frac{e}{2}\left(\det\sigma(t)\right)^{\frac{1}{2n}}\;\gamma
(3.65)
\displaystyle\sigma(0)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\sigma\;,
(3.66)
that always admits a solution.
Therefore as required, if the covariance matrices \sigma_{\alpha} fulfill (3.16) at t=0 , they will fulfill it at any time.
3.3.4 Relative entropy
In order to prove the positivity of the time derivative of the right-hand side of (3.37) along the evolution described in Section 3.3.3, we will link the time derivative of the entropy of a given quantum state to the relative entropy of this state with respect to a displaced version of it.
The relative entropy of a state \hat{\rho} with respect to a state \hat{\sigma} is defined as
S\left(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{\sigma}\right)=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\rho}\left(\ln\hat{\rho}-\ln\hat{\sigma}\right)\right]\;.
(3.67)
The probability of confusing n copies of \hat{\sigma} with n copies of \hat{\rho} scales as \exp\left(-n\,S\left(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{\sigma}\right)\right) in the large n limit [77], so the relative entropy provides a (not symmetric) measure of the distinguishability of two states.
Since any physical operation on states cannot increase distinguishability, the relative entropy decreases under the application of any quantum channel \Phi :
S\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\|\Phi\left(\hat{\sigma}\right)\right)\leq S\left(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{\sigma}\right)\;.
(3.68)
This is called the data-processing inequality.
Many proof of it are known, but none of them is simple.
They can be found in [3, 4, 2].
3.3.5 Quantum Fisher information
The proof of the positivity of the time-derivative of the rate in (3.37) requires the introduction of a quantity that has an importance by its own: the quantum Fisher information.
We define the quantum Fisher information matrix J of a state \hat{\rho} (see [36, 37] for the single mode and [38] for the multimode case) as the Hessian with respect to \mathbf{x} of the relative entropy [2]
S\left(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{\sigma}\right)=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\rho}\left(\ln\hat{\rho}-\ln\hat{\sigma}\right)\right]
(3.69)
between the original state \hat{\rho} and its version displaced by \mathbf{x} :
J_{ij}(\hat{\rho}):=\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}S\left(\hat{\rho}\left\|\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{\rho}\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}\right.\right)\right|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}}\;.
(3.70)
The quantum Fisher information generalizes the classical Fisher information of [32], and measures how much the displaced state \hat{D}(\mathbf{x})\hat{\rho}{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger} is distinguishable from the original one.
For the comparison with the quantum Fisher information of the quantum Cramér-Rao bound [78, 79, 80], see Section A.9 of Appendix A.
We can get a more explicit expression plugging into (3.70) the definition (3.69) of the relative entropy:
J_{ij}(\hat{\rho})=-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{\rho}\;\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})\;\ln\hat{\rho}\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}\right|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}}\right)\;.
(3.71)
3.3.6 De Bruijn identity
The quantum Fisher information is intimately linked to the derivative of the entropy of a state under the evolution induced by the Liouvillian (3.38).
Let us consider indeed an infinitesimal variation
d\hat{\rho}=\mathcal{L}_{d\gamma}(\hat{\rho})\;.
(3.72)
Then, using (3.40) and comparing with (3.71) the variation of the entropy of \hat{\rho} is
dS(\hat{\rho})=\frac{1}{4}d\gamma^{ij}\;J_{ij}(\hat{\rho})\;.
(3.73)
From its classical analog, this equation takes the name of de Bruijn identity.
3.3.7 Stam inequality
The positivity the derivative of the rate (3.37) will follow from an inequality on the quantum Fisher information, called quantum Stam inequality from its classical analog [32, 81].
The core of its proof is the data-processing inequality for the relative entropy [2], stating that it decreases under the action of any completely-positive trace-preserving map:
\displaystyle S\left(\hat{\rho}\left\|\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{\rho}\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}\right.\right)
\displaystyle\geq
\displaystyle S\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\left\|\Phi\left(\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{\rho}\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}\right)\right.\right)=
(3.74)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle S\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\left\|\hat{D}\left(M\mathbf{x}\right)\;\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\;{\hat{D}\left(M\mathbf{x}\right)}^{\dagger}\right.\right)\;,
where we have used (3.46).
Since both members of (3.74) are always nonnegative and vanish for \mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0} , this point is a minimum for both, and the inequality translates to the Hessians:
\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}S\left(\hat{\rho}\left\|\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{\rho}\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}\right.\right)\right|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}}
\displaystyle\geq
\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}S\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\left\|\hat{D}\left(M\mathbf{x}\right)\;\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\;{\hat{D}\left(M\mathbf{x}\right)}^{\dagger}\right.\right)\right|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}}=
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left.M^{k}_{\phantom{k}i}M^{l}_{\phantom{l}j}\;\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{k}\partial y^{l}}S\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\left\|\hat{D}(\mathbf{y})\;\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{y})}^{\dagger}\right.\right)\right|_{\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{0}}\;,
(3.75)
where the inequalities are meant for the whole matrices (and not for their entries), and we have made the change of variable
\mathbf{y}=M\mathbf{x}\;.
(3.76)
Recalling the definition of Fisher information matrix (3.70), inequality (3.75) becomes
J\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\geq M^{T}\;J\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\right)\;M\;.
(3.77)
Inequality (3.77) is equivalent to
{J\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\right)}^{-1}\geq M\;{J\left(\hat{\rho}\right)}^{-1}\;M^{T}\;.
(3.78)
To see this, it is sufficient to choose bases in X and Y such that
\displaystyle J\left(\hat{\rho}\right)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{X}
(3.79)
\displaystyle J\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\right)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{Y}\;.
(3.80)
Then, (3.77) and (3.78) read
\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{X}
\displaystyle\geq
\displaystyle M^{T}\;M
(3.81)
\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{Y}
\displaystyle\geq
\displaystyle M\;M^{T}\;,
(3.82)
that are equivalent since M^{T}\,M and M\,M^{T} have the same spectrum, except for the multiplicity of the eigenvalue zero.
Inequality (3.78) is called the quantum Stam inequality.
In the particular case of the beamsplitter, it has already appeared in [36, 37], while in the multimode scenario it is an original result of this Thesis.
3.3.8 Positivity of the time-derivative
We have now all the instruments to prove that the time-derivative of the rate (3.37) is positive.
Recalling the definition (3.58), we can write the inequality to be proved as
\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\lambda_{\alpha}\;\mu_{\alpha}(t)}{\mu_{Y}(t)}\geq 0\;.
(3.83)
Let us now define the functions
\displaystyle J_{\alpha}(t)
\displaystyle:=
\displaystyle J\left(\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}(t)\right)
(3.84)
\displaystyle J_{Y}(t)
\displaystyle:=
\displaystyle J\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}(t)\right)\right)\;.
(3.85)
Combining the de Bruijn identity (3.73) and the definition of the time evolution in (3.51) and (3.57), the time-derivative of the entropy of each input can be linked to its quantum Fisher information matrix:
\frac{d}{dt}S\left(\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}(t)\right)=\frac{\mu_{\alpha}(t)}{4}\;\gamma_{\alpha}^{ij}\;J^{\alpha}_{ij}(t)\;,
(3.86)
and consequently
\frac{d}{dt}\mu_{\alpha}(t)=\frac{{\mu_{\alpha}(t)}^{2}}{4n}\;\gamma_{\alpha}^{ij}\;J^{\alpha}_{ij}(t)\;.
(3.87)
With also (3.52) and (3.53), the analog for the output is
\frac{d}{dt}S\left(\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}(t)\right)\right)=\frac{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\lambda_{\alpha}\;\mu_{\alpha}(t)}{4}\;\gamma^{ij}\;J^{Y}_{ij}(t)\;,
(3.88)
and
\frac{d}{dt}\mu_{Y}(t)=\mu_{Y}(t)\;\frac{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\lambda_{\alpha}\;\mu_{\alpha}(t)}{4n}\;\gamma^{ij}\;J^{Y}_{ij}(t)\;.
(3.89)
Then (3.83) becomes
\left(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\lambda_{\alpha}\;\mu_{\alpha}(t)\right)^{2}\gamma^{ij}\;J^{Y}_{ij}(t)\leq\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\lambda_{\alpha}\;{\mu_{\alpha}(t)}^{2}\;\gamma^{ij}_{\alpha}\;J^{\alpha}_{ij}(t)\;.
(3.90)
To prove (3.90), we use the quantum Stam inequality in the form (3.77), that for our K -partite input reads
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}J_{1}(t)&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&J_{K}(t)\\
\end{array}\right)\geq\left(\begin{array}[]{c}M_{1}^{T}\\
\vdots\\
M_{K}^{T}\\
\end{array}\right)\;J_{Y}(t)\;\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}M_{1}&\ldots&M_{K}\\
\end{array}\right)\;.
(3.91)
Multiplying on the left by \left(\mu_{1}(t)\lambda_{1}M_{1}^{-T}\;\ldots\;\mu_{K}(t)\lambda_{K}M_{K}^{-T}\right) and on the right by its transpose, we get
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\lambda_{\alpha}^{2}\;{\mu_{\alpha}(t)}^{2}\;M_{\alpha}^{T}\;J_{\alpha}(t)\;M_{\alpha}^{-1}\geq\left(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\lambda_{\alpha}\;\mu_{\alpha}(t)\right)^{2}J_{Y}(t)\;,
(3.92)
and (3.90) follows upon taking the trace with \gamma and recalling (3.49).
3.3.9 Asymptotic scaling
In this Section we show that the rate (3.37) tends to 1 for t\to\infty , concluding then the proof of the EPI.
For this purpose, we first prove that for any strictly positive matrix \gamma>0 the entropy of e^{t\;\mathcal{L}(\gamma)}(\hat{\rho}) for t\to\infty is asymptotically
S\left(e^{t\;\mathcal{L}(\gamma)}(\hat{\rho})\right)=n\ln\frac{t}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\ln\det\gamma+n+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\;.
(3.93)
A lower bound for the entropy
A lower bound for the entropy follows on expressing the state \hat{\rho} in terms of its generalized Husimi function (see Section A.6 of Appendix A).
We define
t_{1}=\frac{1}{\nu_{\min}}\;,
(3.94)
where \nu_{\min} is the minimum symplectic eigenvalue of \gamma . We have then
t_{1}\,\gamma\geq\pm i\,\Delta\;,
(3.95)
and we can exploit the generalized Husimi representation (A.51) associated to the matrix t_{1}\,\gamma :
\hat{\rho}=\int Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{\rho}_{G}(-t_{1}\gamma,\,\mathbf{x})\;d^{2n}x\;.
(3.96)
For the linearity of the evolution (3.38), we can take the super-operator e^{t\;\mathcal{L}(\gamma)} inside the integral, and remembering (3.45) we get
e^{t\;\mathcal{L}(\gamma)}(\hat{\rho})=\int Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{\rho}_{G}((t-t_{1})\gamma,\,\mathbf{x})\;d^{2n}x\;.
(3.97)
For t\geq 2t_{1} , we have
(t-t_{1})\gamma\geq t_{1}\,\gamma\geq\pm i\Delta\;,
(3.98)
i.e. \hat{\rho}_{G}((t-t_{1})\gamma) is a proper quantum state.
Since Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x}) is a probability distribution, the concavity of the von Neumann entropy implies
S\left(e^{t\;\mathcal{L}(\gamma)}(\hat{\rho})\right)\geq S\left(\hat{\rho}_{G}((t-t_{1})\gamma)\right)=n\ln\frac{t}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\ln\det\gamma+n+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\;,
(3.99)
where we have used Eq. (A.41) of Appendix A.
An upper bound for the entropy
Given a state \hat{\rho} , let \hat{\rho}_{G} be the centered Gaussian state with the same covariance matrix. It is then possible to prove [66] that S\left(\hat{\rho}_{G}\right)\geq S\left(\hat{\rho}\right) .
Let \sigma be the covariance matrix of \hat{\rho} , respectively. Then, (3.43) implies
\left(e^{t\;\mathcal{L}(\gamma)}\hat{\rho}\right)_{G}=\hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma+t\gamma)\;,
(3.100)
so that
S\left(e^{t\;\mathcal{L}(\gamma)}\hat{\rho}\right)\leq S\left(\hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma+t\gamma)\right)\;.
(3.101)
Let t_{2} be the maximum eigenvalue of \sigma\,\gamma^{-1} ( \gamma and \sigma are strictly positive, so \gamma^{-1} exists and t_{2} is finite and strictly positive). Then,
\sigma\leq t_{2}\,\gamma
(3.102)
(to see this, it is sufficient to choose a basis in which \gamma=\mathbb{I}_{2n} ).
We remind that given two covariance matrices \sigma^{\prime}\leq\sigma^{\prime\prime} , the Gaussian state \hat{\rho}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}} can be obtained applying an additive noise channel to \hat{\rho}_{\sigma^{\prime}} . Since such channel is unital, it always increases the entropy, so we have S(\hat{\rho}_{\sigma^{\prime}})\leq S(\hat{\rho}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}) . Applying this to \sigma+t\gamma\leq(t_{2}+t)\gamma , we get again
S\left(\hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma+t\gamma)\right)\leq S\left(\hat{\rho}_{G}((t_{2}+t)\gamma)\right)=n\ln\frac{t}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\ln\det\gamma+n+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\;,
(3.103)
where we have used (A.41) again.
Scaling of the rate
From Section 3.3.3 we can see that for our evolutions if M_{\alpha} is invertible \det\gamma_{\alpha}=1 , so
\mu_{\alpha}(t)=\frac{e}{2}t_{\alpha}(t)+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)\;,
(3.104)
and similarly
\mu_{Y}(t)=\frac{e}{2}t_{Y}(t)+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)\;.
(3.105)
Replacing this into (3.37), and remembering that if M_{\alpha} is not invertible, then \lambda_{\alpha}=0 and the corresponding terms vanish,
from (3.53) it easily follows that such quantity tends to 1 in the t\rightarrow\infty limit.
3.4 The Entropy Photon-number Inequality
The quantum EPI (3.35) is not saturated by Gaussian states with proportional covariance matrices, and then it is not sufficient to determine the minimum entropy of \mathbf{Y} for fixed entropies of \mathbf{X}_{1} and \mathbf{X}_{2} .
However, as in the classical case, Gaussian states with proportional covariance matrices are conjectured to be the solution to this optimization problem.
This belief has led to conjecture the Entropy Photon number Inequality (EPnI) [27, 28]:
N(\hat{\rho}_{Y})\overset{?}{\geq}\lambda\;N(\hat{\rho}_{1})+(1-\lambda)\;N(\hat{\rho}_{2})\;.
(3.106)
Here
g(N)=(N+1)\ln(N+1)-N\ln N
(3.107)
is the entropy of a single mode thermal Gaussian state with mean photon number N (see Section A.5.1 of Appendix A), and
N(\hat{\rho})=g^{-1}\left(S(\hat{\rho})/n\right)
(3.108)
is the mean photon number per mode of an n -mode thermal Gaussian state with the same entropy of \hat{\rho} .
Indeed, the EPnI states exactly that fixing the input entropies S_{1} , S_{2} , the output entropy S_{Y} is minimum when the inputs are Gaussian with proportional covariance matrices.
Since the qEPI (3.35) is not saturated by Gaussian states with proportional covariance matrices (unless they have also the same entropy), it is weaker than (and it is actually implied by) the EPnI (3.106), so our proof of qEPI does not imply the EPnI, which still remains an open conjecture.
We show in Section A.8 of Appendix A that the EPnI (3.106) holds for any couple of n -mode Gaussian states.
In Chapter 5 we prove the EPnI (3.106) in the one-mode case when the second input is chosen to be the vacuum.
Moreover, as we are going to show, the validity of the qEPI imposes a very tight bound (of the order of 0.132 ) on the maximum allowed violation of the EPnI (3.106).
The map e^{S(\hat{\rho})/n}\mapsto N(\hat{\rho}) from the entropy power to the entropy photon-number is the function f(x)\equiv g^{-1}(\ln(x)) defined on the interval [1,\infty] . Unfortunately it is convex and we cannot
obtain the EPnI (3.106) from (3.35). Fortunately however, f(x) is not too convex and is well approximated by a linear function. It is easy to show indeed (see Section 3.4.1) that
f(x)=-1/2+x/e+\delta(x)\;,
(3.109)
where
0\leq\delta(x)\leq\delta(1)=1/2-1/e\simeq 0.132\;.
(3.110)
This directly implies that the entropy photon number inequality is valid up to such a small error,
N(\rho_{Y})-\lambda\;N(\rho_{1})-(1-\lambda)\;N(\rho_{2})\geq 1/e-1/2\;.
(3.111)
We also conjecture that the EPnI can be extended to the most general multimode scenario, i.e. that upon fixing the entropy of each input of the channel defined in (3.20), the output entropy is still minimized by Gaussian input states.
This multimode EPnI would be an improvement of our EPI (3.33), since it would imply it.
However, it cannot be written with elementary functions as an inequality on the output entropy, since the optimization over all the Gaussian input states cannot be performed analytically.
3.4.1 Proof of the bound
We want to evaluate how close is our qEPI (3.35) to the EPnI (3.106) and prove (3.111).
The qEPI (3.35) implies for the output entropy photon number
N_{Y}\geq g^{-1}\left(\ln\left(\lambda\;e^{g(N_{1})}+(1-\lambda)\;e^{g(N_{2})}\right)\right)\;.
(3.112)
The EPnI (3.106) is stronger than the EPI (3.35), and in fact
g^{-1}\left(\ln\left(\lambda\;e^{g(N_{1})}+(1-\lambda)\;e^{g(N_{2})}\right)\right)\leq\lambda\;N_{1}+(1-\lambda)\;N_{2}\;,
(3.113)
since the function g^{-1}\left(\ln\left(x\right)\right) is increasing and convex.
Since e^{g(N)} for N\to\infty goes like
e^{g(N)}=e\left(N+\frac{1}{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\;,
(3.114)
we have for x\to\infty
g^{-1}\left(\ln x\right)=\frac{x}{e}-\frac{1}{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)\;.
(3.115)
If we define
\delta(x)\equiv g^{-1}\left(\ln x\right)-\frac{x}{e}+\frac{1}{2}\;,
(3.116)
\delta is convex, decreasing and
\lim_{x\to\infty}\delta(x)=0\;.
(3.117)
We can also evaluate
\delta(1)=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{e}\;,
(3.118)
and for any x_{1},\;x_{2}\geq 1 we have
\delta(\lambda\;x_{1}+(1-\lambda)\;x_{2})\geq\lambda\;\delta(x_{1})+(1-\lambda)\;\delta(x_{2})-\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{e}\right)\;.
(3.119)
Since
\displaystyle g^{-1}\left(\ln\left(\lambda\;x_{1}+(1-\lambda)\;x_{2}\right)\right)-\lambda\;g^{-1}\left(\ln x_{1}\right)-(1-\lambda)\;g^{-1}\left(\ln x_{2}\right)=
\displaystyle=\delta\left(\lambda\;x_{1}+(1-\lambda)\;x_{2}\right)-\lambda\;\delta(x_{1})-(1-\lambda)\;\delta(x_{2})\;,
(3.120)
in the case x_{1}=e^{S_{1}} , x_{2}=e^{S_{2}} we get
\displaystyle g^{-1}\left(\ln\left(\lambda\;e^{S_{1}}+(1-\lambda)\;e^{S_{2}}\right)\right)-\lambda\;N_{1}-(1-\lambda)\;N_{2}=
\displaystyle=\delta\left(\lambda\;e^{S_{1}}+(1-\lambda)\;e^{S_{2}}\right)-\lambda\;\delta(e^{S_{1}})-(1-\lambda)\;\delta(e^{S_{2}})\;,
(3.121)
and we can conclude from (3.112) that
\displaystyle N_{Y}
\displaystyle\geq
\displaystyle\lambda\;N_{1}+(1-\lambda)\;N_{2}+\delta(\lambda\;e^{S_{1}}+(1-\lambda)\;e^{S_{2}})-\lambda\;\delta(e^{S_{1}})-(1-\lambda)\;\delta(e^{S_{2}})\geq
(3.122)
\displaystyle\geq
\displaystyle\lambda\;N_{1}+(1-\lambda)\;N_{2}-\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{e}\right)\;,
so the (3.106) violation can be at most
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{e}\simeq 0.132\;.
(3.123)
3.5 The constrained minimum output entropy conjecture
Recently the so called minimum output entropy conjecture has been proved (see [16, 18, 14] and Section 2.6).
It claims that the output entropy of a gauge-covariant Gaussian channel is minimum when the input is the vacuum.
A large class of physically relevant gauge-covariant Gaussian channels can be constructed with the beamsplitter defined in Eqs. (3.27) and (3.28) taking as second input \hat{\rho}_{2} a fixed Gaussian thermal state.
In this setup, the EPnI implies that the entropy of the output S_{Y} is minimum when the first input \hat{\rho}_{1} is the vacuum, i.e. the MOE conjecture.
A more general problem [28, 27] is to determine what is the minimum output entropy S_{Y} with the constraint that the entropy of the first input S_{1} is fixed to some value \bar{S}>0 .
For simplicity, we concentrate on the one-mode case and we fix the second input to be the vacuum:
\hat{\rho}_{2}=|0\rangle\langle 0|\;.
(3.124)
It is easy to show that the EPnI (3.106) implies that the minimum of S_{Y} is achieved by the Gaussian thermal state with entropy \bar{S} , corresponding to an output entropy of
S_{Y}=g\left(\lambda\;g^{-1}\left(\bar{S}\right)\right)\;.
(3.125)
We will prove (3.125) in Chapter 5.
Here we use our qEPI to obtain a tight lower bound
on S_{Y} . The bound follows directly from (3.35) for S_{2}=0 and can be expressed as
S_{Y}\geq\ln\left[\lambda\;e^{\bar{S}}+(1-\lambda)\right]\;.
(3.126)
The RHS of (3.126) is extremely close to the conjectured minimum g\left(\lambda g^{-1}\left(\bar{S}\right)\right) . Indeed the error between the two quantities
\Delta(\bar{S},\lambda)=g\left(\lambda g^{-1}\left(\bar{S}\right)\right)-\ln\left[\lambda e^{\bar{S}}+(1-\lambda)\right]
(3.127)
is bounded by \sim 0.107 and moreover it decays to zero in large part of the parameter space (\bar{S},\lambda) (see Fig. 3.3).
Figure 3.3: a Plot of the output entropies as functions of \lambda and for different input entropies \bar{S}=0.5,1,1.5 . In full lines are the entropy achievable with a Gaussian input state while the dotted lines represent the lower bound (3.126). The corresponding minimum output entropies are necessarily constrained within the green regions. We notice that larger values of input entropies \bar{S} are not considered in this plot because the Gaussian ansatz and the bound becomes practically indistinguishable. b Maximum allowed violation \Delta(\bar{S},\lambda) of the generalized minimum output entropy conjecture. The two axes are the input entropy \bar{S} and the beamsplitter transmissivity \lambda .
3.6 Conclusion
Understanding the complex physics of continuous variable quantum systems [8] represents a fundamental challenge of modern science which is crucial
for developing an information technology capable of taking full advantage of quantum effects [6, 9].
This task appears now to be within our grasp due to a series of very recent works which have solved a collection of long standing conjectures. Specifically,
the minimum output entropy and output majorization conjectures (proposed in Ref. [15] and solved in Ref.’s [16] and [18] respectively),
the optimal Gaussian ensemble and the additivity conjecture (proposed in [67] and solved in Ref. [16]),
the optimality of Gaussian decomposition in the calculation of entanglement of formation [82] and of Gaussian discord [83, 84]
for two-mode gaussian states (both solved in Ref. [14]), the proof of the strong converse of the classical capacity theorem [85].
This result represents a fundamental further step in this direction by extending the proof of [36] for the qEPI conjecture to the most general multimode scenario.
Chapter 4 Optimal inputs: passive states
The passive states of a quantum system minimize the average energy for fixed spectrum.
In this Chapter we prove that these states are the optimal inputs of one-mode gauge-covariant Gaussian quantum channels, in the sense that the output generated by a passive state majorizes the output generated by any other state with the same spectrum.
This result reduces the constrained quantum minimum output entropy conjecture (Proposition 2.6) to a problem on discrete classical probability distributions.
The Chapter is based on
[45]
G. De Palma, D. Trevisan, and V. Giovannetti, “Passive States Optimize the
Output of Bosonic Gaussian Quantum Channels,” IEEE Transactions on
Information Theory, vol. 62, no. 5, pp. 2895–2906, May 2016.
http://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/7442587
4.1 Introduction
The minimum von Neumann entropy at the output of a quantum communication channel can be crucial for the determination of its classical communication capacity (see [2] and Section 2.6).
Most communication schemes encode the information into pulses of electromagnetic radiation, that travels through metal wires, optical fibers or free space and is unavoidably affected by attenuation and noise.
The gauge-covariant quantum Gaussian channels [2] presented in Chapter 2 provide a faithful model for these effects, and are characterized by the property of preserving the thermal states of electromagnetic radiation.
It has been recently proved (see [18, 16, 17, 11] and Section 2.6) that the output entropy of any gauge-covariant Gaussian quantum channel is minimized when the input state is the vacuum.
This result has permitted the determination of the classical information capacity of this class of channels [14].
However, it is not sufficient to determine the triple trade-off region of the same class of channels [25, 26], nor the capacity region of the Gaussian quantum broadcast channel.
Indeed, the solutions of these problems both rely on the still unproven constrained minimum output entropy conjecture 2.6, stating that Gaussian thermal input states minimize the output von Neumann entropy of a quantum-limited attenuator among all the states with a given entropy (see [21, 22] and Sections 2.8, 2.9 and 2.10).
This still unproven result would follow from a stronger conjecture, the Entropy Photon-number Inequality (EPnI) (see [28] and Section 3.4), stating that Gaussian states with proportional covariance matrices minimize the output von Neumann entropy of a beamsplitter among all the couples of input states, each one with a given entropy.
Actually, Ref.’s [18, 17, 11] do not only prove that the vacuum minimizes the output entropy of any gauge-covariant quantum Gaussian channel.
They also prove that the output generated by the vacuum majorizes the output generated by any other state, i.e. applying a convex combination of unitary operators to the former, we can obtain any of the latter states (see Section 2.7).
In this Chapter we go in the same direction, and prove a generalization of this result valid for any one-mode gauge-covariant quantum Gaussian channel.
Our result states that the output generated by any quantum state is majorized by the output generated by the state with the same spectrum diagonal in the Fock basis and with decreasing eigenvalues, i.e. by the state which is passive [39, 40, 41] with respect to the number operator (see [42, 43, 44] for the use of passive states in the context of quantum thermodynamics).
This can be understood as follows: among all the states with a given spectrum, the one diagonal in the Fock basis with decreasing eigenvalues produces the less noisy output.
All the states with a given spectrum have the same von Neumann entropy.
Then, our result implies that the input state minimizing the output entropy for fixed input entropy is certainly diagonal in the Fock basis.
This reduces the minimum output entropy quantum problem to a problem on discrete classical probability distributions.
We will solve this reduced problem in Chapter 5, where we prove that Gaussian thermal input states minimize the output entropy of the one-mode quantum attenuator for fixed input entropy.
Thanks to the classification of one-mode Gaussian channels in terms of unitary equivalence [86, 2], we extend the result of this Chapter to the channels that are not gauge-covariant with the exception of the singular cases A_{2}) and B_{1}) , for which we show that an optimal basis does not exist.
We also point out that the classical channel acting on discrete probability distributions associated to the restriction of the quantum-limited attenuator to states diagonal in the Fock basis coincides with the channel already known in the probability literature under the name of thinning.
First introduced by Rényi [87] as a discrete analog of the rescaling of a continuous random variable, the thinning has been recently involved in discrete versions of the central limit theorem [88, 89, 90]
and of the Entropy Power Inequality [91, 92].
In particular, the Restricted Thinned Entropy Power Inequality [92] states that the Poisson probability distribution minimizes the output Shannon entropy of the thinning among all the ultra log-concave input probability distributions with a given Shannon entropy.
The Chapter is organized as follows.
In Section 4.2 we introduce the Gaussian quantum channels.
The Fock rearrangement is defined in Section 4.3, while Section 4.4 defines the notion of Fock optimality and proves some of its properties.
The main theorem is proved in Section 4.5, and the case of a generic not gauge-covariant Gaussian channel is treated in Section 4.6.
Section 4.7 links our result to the thinning operation, and we conclude in Section 4.8.
4.2 Preliminaries
In this Section we recall some properties of Gaussian quantum channels.
For more details, see Sections 2.3 and A.7, and the books [2, 60, 93].
We consider a one-mode Gaussian quantum system (see Section 2.1), i.e. the Hilbert space \mathcal{H} of one harmonic oscillator.
\mathcal{H} has a countable orthonormal basis
\{|n\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\;,\qquad\langle m|n\rangle=\delta_{mn}
(4.1)
called the Fock basis, on which the ladder operator \hat{a} acts as
\hat{a}\;|n\rangle=\sqrt{n}\;|n-1\rangle\;,\qquad\hat{a}^{\dagger}\;|n\rangle=\sqrt{n+1}\;|n+1\rangle\;.
(4.2)
For one mode, the Hamiltonian (2.10) reduces to
\hat{N}=\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\;,
(4.3)
satisfying
\hat{N}\;|n\rangle=n\;|n\rangle\;.
(4.4)
Lemma 4.1.
The quantum-limited attenuator of parameter 0\leq\lambda\leq 1 (see Section 2.3.1) admits the explicit representation
\Phi_{\lambda}\left(\hat{X}\right)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{(1-\lambda)^{l}}{l!}\;\lambda^{\frac{\hat{N}}{2}}\;\hat{a}^{l}\;\hat{X}\;\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right)^{l}\;\lambda^{\frac{\hat{N}}{2}}
(4.5)
for any trace-class operator \hat{X} .
Then, if \hat{X} is diagonal in the Fock basis, \Phi_{\lambda}\left(\hat{X}\right) is diagonal in the same basis for any 0\leq\lambda\leq 1 also.
Proof.
The channel \Phi_{\lambda} admits the Kraus decomposition (see Eq. (4.5) of [94])
\Phi_{\lambda}\left(\hat{X}\right)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\hat{B}_{l}\;\hat{X}\;\hat{B}_{l}^{\dagger}\;,
(4.6)
where
\hat{B}_{l}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\binom{m+l}{l}}\;(1-\lambda)^{\frac{l}{2}}\;\lambda^{\frac{m}{2}}\;|m\rangle\langle m+l|\;,\qquad l\in\mathbb{N}\;.
(4.7)
Using (4.2), we have
\hat{a}^{l}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{l!\;\binom{m+l}{l}}\;|m\rangle\langle m+l|\;,
(4.8)
and the claim easily follows.
∎
Lemma 4.2.
The quantum-limited attenuator of parameter \lambda=e^{-t} with t\geq 0 can be written as the exponential of a Lindbladian \mathcal{L} , i.e. \Phi_{\lambda}=e^{t\mathcal{L}} , where
\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{X}\right)=\hat{a}\;\hat{X}\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\;\hat{X}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{X}\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}
(4.9)
for any trace-class operator \hat{X} .
Proof.
Putting \lambda=e^{-t} into (4.5) and differentiating with respect to t we have for any trace-class operator \hat{X}
\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{\lambda}\left(\hat{X}\right)=\mathcal{L}\left(\Phi_{\lambda}\left(\hat{X}\right)\right)\;,
(4.10)
where \mathcal{L} is the Lindbladian given by (4.9).
∎
Lemma 4.3.
Let
\hat{X}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x_{k}\;|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|\;,\quad\langle\psi_{k}|\psi_{l}\rangle=\delta_{kl}\;,\quad x_{0}\geq x_{1}\geq\ldots
(4.11)
be a self-adjoint Hilbert-Schmidt operator.
Then, the projectors
\hat{\Pi}_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|
(4.12)
satisfy
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}\;\hat{X}\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{n}x_{k}\;.
(4.13)
Proof.
Easily follows from an explicit computation.
∎
Lemma 4.4 (Ky Fan’s Maximum Principle).
Let \hat{X} be a positive Hilbert-Schmidt operator with eigenvalues \{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}} in decreasing order, i.e. x_{0}\geq x_{1}\geq\ldots\; ,
and let \hat{P} be a projector of rank n+1 .
Then
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}\;\hat{X}\right]\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n}x_{k}\;.
(4.14)
Proof.
(See also [95, 96]).
Let us diagonalize \hat{X} as in (4.11).
The proof proceeds by induction on n .
Let \hat{P} have rank one.
Since
\hat{X}\leq x_{0}\;\hat{\mathbb{I}}\;,
(4.15)
we have
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}\;\hat{X}\right]\leq x_{0}\;.
(4.16)
Suppose now that (4.14) holds for any rank- n projector.
Let \hat{P} be a projector of rank n+1 .
Its support then certainly contains a vector |\psi\rangle orthogonal to the support of \hat{\Pi}_{n-1} , that has rank n .
We can choose |\psi\rangle normalized (i.e. \langle\psi|\psi\rangle=1 ), and define the rank- n projector
\hat{Q}=\hat{P}-|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|\;.
(4.17)
By the induction hypothesis on \hat{Q} ,
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}\,\hat{X}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}\,\hat{X}\right]+\langle\psi|\hat{X}|\psi\rangle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}x_{k}+\langle\psi|\hat{X}|\psi\rangle\;.
(4.18)
Since |\psi\rangle is in the support of \hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{\Pi}_{n-1} , and
\left(\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{\Pi}_{n-1}\right)\hat{X}\left(\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{\Pi}_{n-1}\right)\leq x_{n}\;\hat{\mathbb{I}}\;,
(4.19)
we have
\langle\psi|\hat{X}|\psi\rangle\leq x_{n}\;,
(4.20)
and this concludes the proof.
∎
Lemma 4.5.
Let \hat{X} and \hat{Y} be positive Hilbert-Schmidt operators (see (A.11) in Appendix A) with eigenvalues in decreasing order \{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} and \{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} , respectively.
Then,
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(x_{n}-y_{n})^{2}\leq\left\|\hat{X}-\hat{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}\;.
(4.21)
Proof.
We have
\left\|\hat{X}-\hat{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(x_{n}-y_{n})^{2}=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}y_{n}-2\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{X}\hat{Y}\right]\geq 0\,.
(4.22)
To prove the inequality in (4.22), let us diagonalize \hat{X} as in (4.11).
We then also have
\hat{X}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(x_{n}-x_{n+1}\right)\hat{\Pi}_{n}\;,
(4.23)
where
\hat{\Pi}_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}|\psi_{k}\rangle\langle\psi_{k}|\;.
(4.24)
We then have
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{X}\;\hat{Y}\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(x_{n}-x_{n+1}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}\;\hat{Y}\right]\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(x_{n}-x_{n+1}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{n}y_{k}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}\;y_{n}\;,
(4.25)
where we have used Ky Fan’s Maximum Principle (Lemma 4.4) and rearranged the sum (see also the Supplemental Material of [97]).
∎
4.3 Fock rearrangement
In order to state our main theorem, we need the following:
Definition 4.6 (Fock rearrangement).
Let \hat{X} be a positive trace-class operator with eigenvalues \{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} in decreasing order.
We define its Fock rearrangement as
\hat{X}^{\downarrow}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}\;|n\rangle\langle n|\;.
(4.26)
If \hat{X} coincides with its own Fock rearrangement, i.e. \hat{X}=\hat{X}^{\downarrow} , we say that it is passive [39, 40, 41] with respect to the Hamiltonian \hat{N} .
For simplicity, in the following we will always assume \hat{N} to be the reference Hamiltonian, and an operator with \hat{X}=\hat{X}^{\downarrow} will be called simply passive.
Remark 4.7.
The Fock rearrangement of any projector \hat{\Pi}_{n} of rank n+1 is the projector onto the first n+1 Fock states:
\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}|i\rangle\langle i|\;.
(4.27)
We recall that a quantum operation has the same definition of a quantum channel, but it is not required to be trace-preserving (see Section 2.3).
We define the notion of passive-preserving quantum operation, that will be useful in the following.
Definition 4.8 (Passive-preserving quantum operation).
We say that a quantum operation \Phi is passive-preserving if \Phi\left(\hat{X}\right) is passive for any passive positive trace-class operator \hat{X} .
We will also need these lemmata:
Lemma 4.9.
For any self-adjoint trace-class operator \hat{X} ,
\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{X}\;\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}-\hat{X}\right\|_{2}=0\;,
(4.28)
where the \hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow} are the projectors onto the first N+1 Fock states defined in (4.27).
Proof.
We have
\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\hat{X}\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}-\hat{X}\right\|_{2}^{2}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{X}\left(\hat{\mathbb{I}}+\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\right)\hat{X}\left(\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\right)\right]\leq 2\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{X}^{2}\left(\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\right)\right]=
(4.29)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle 2\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}\langle n|\hat{X}^{2}|n\rangle\;,
where we have used that \hat{\mathbb{I}}+\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\leq 2\;\hat{\mathbb{I}}\, .
Since \hat{X} has finite trace-norm, also its Hilbert-Schmidt norm is finite, the sum in (4.29) converges, and its tail tends to zero for N\to\infty .
∎
Lemma 4.10.
A positive trace-class operator \hat{X} is passive iff for any finite-rank projector \hat{P}
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}\;\hat{X}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{X}\right]\;.
(4.30)
Proof.
First, let us suppose that \hat{X} is passive with eigenvalues \{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} in decreasing order, and let \hat{P} have rank n+1 .
Then, by Lemma 4.4
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}\;\hat{X}\right]\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{X}\right]\;.
(4.31)
Let us now suppose that (4.30) holds for any finite-rank projector.
Let us diagonalize \hat{X} as in (4.11).
Putting into (4.30) the projectors \hat{\Pi}_{n} defined in (4.12),
\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}\;\hat{X}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{X}\right]\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}\;,
(4.32)
where we have again used Lemma 4.4.
It follows that for any n\in\mathbb{N}
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{X}\right]=\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}\;,
(4.33)
and
\langle n|\hat{X}|n\rangle=x_{n}\;.
(4.34)
It is then easy to prove by induction on n that
\hat{X}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}\;|n\rangle\langle n|\;,
(4.35)
i.e. \hat{X} is passive.
∎
Lemma 4.11.
Let \left\{\hat{X}_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} be a sequence of positive trace-class operators with \hat{X}_{n} passive for any n\in\mathbb{N} .
Then also \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{X}_{n} is passive, provided that its trace is finite.
Proof.
Follows easily from the definition of Fock rearrangement.
∎
Lemma 4.12.
Let \Phi be a quantum operation.
Let us suppose that \Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}\right) is passive for any passive finite-rank projector \hat{\Pi} .
Then, \Phi is passive-preserving.
Proof.
Choose a passive operator
\hat{X}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}\,|n\rangle\langle n|\;,
(4.36)
with \{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} positive and decreasing.
We then also have
\hat{X}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}\;\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;,
(4.37)
where the \hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow} are defined in (4.27), and
z_{n}=x_{n}-x_{n+1}\geq 0\;.
(4.38)
Since by hypothesis \Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\right) is passive for any n\in\mathbb{N} , according to Lemma 4.11 also
\Phi\left(\hat{X}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}\;\Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\right)
(4.39)
is passive.
∎
Lemma 4.13.
Let \hat{X} and \hat{Y} be positive trace-class operators.
1.
Let us suppose that for any finite-rank projector \hat{\Pi}
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}\,\hat{X}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}^{\downarrow}\,\hat{Y}\right]\;.
(4.40)
Then \hat{X}\prec_{w}\hat{Y} (see Section 2.7 for the definition of weak submajorization).
2.
Let \hat{Y} be passive, and let us suppose that \hat{X}\prec_{w}\hat{Y} .
Then (4.40) holds for any finite-rank projector \hat{\Pi} .
Proof.
Let \{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} and \{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} be the eigenvalues in decreasing order of \hat{X} and \hat{Y} , respectively, and let us diagonalize \hat{X} as in (4.11).
1.
Let us first suppose that (4.40) holds for any finite-rank projector \hat{\Pi} .
For any n\in\mathbb{N} we have
\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}\,\hat{X}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\,\hat{Y}\right]\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n}y_{i}\;,
(4.41)
where the \hat{\Pi}_{n} are defined in (4.12) and we have used Lemma 4.4.
Then x\prec_{w}y , and \hat{X}\prec_{w}\hat{Y} .
2.
Let us now suppose that \hat{X}\prec_{w}\hat{Y} and \hat{Y}=\hat{Y}^{\downarrow} .
Then, for any n\in\mathbb{N} and any projector \hat{\Pi} of rank n+1 ,
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}\,\hat{X}\right]\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n}y_{i}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}^{\downarrow}\,\hat{Y}\right]\;,
(4.42)
where we have used Lemma 4.4 again.
∎
Lemma 4.14.
Let \hat{Y} and \hat{Z} be positive trace-class operators with \hat{Y}\prec_{w}\hat{Z}=\hat{Z}^{\downarrow} .
Then, for any positive trace-class operator \hat{X} ,
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{X}\;\hat{Y}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{X}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{Z}\right]\;.
(4.43)
Proof.
Let us diagonalize \hat{X} as in (4.11).
Then, it can be rewritten as
\hat{X}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}\,\hat{\Pi}_{n}\;,
(4.44)
where the projectors \hat{\Pi}_{n} are as in (4.12) and
d_{n}=x_{n}-x_{n+1}\geq 0\;.
(4.45)
The Fock rearrangement of \hat{X} is
\hat{X}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}\,\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;.
(4.46)
We then have from Lemma 4.13
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{X}\;\hat{Y}\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}\;\hat{Y}\right]\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{Z}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{X}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{Z}\right]\;.
(4.47)
∎
Lemma 4.15.
Let \left\{\hat{X}_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} and \left\{\hat{Y}_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} be two sequences of positive trace-class operators, with \hat{Y}_{n}=\hat{Y}_{n}^{\downarrow} and \hat{X}_{n}\prec_{w}\hat{Y}_{n} for any n\in\mathbb{N} .
Then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{X}_{n}\prec_{w}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{Y}_{n}\;,
(4.48)
provided that both sides have finite traces.
Proof.
Let \hat{P} be a finite-rank projector.
Since \hat{X}_{n}\prec_{w}\hat{Y}_{n} and Y_{n}=Y_{n}^{\downarrow} , by the second part of Lemma 4.13
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}\;\hat{X}_{n}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{Y}_{n}\right]\qquad\forall\;n\in\mathbb{N}\;.
(4.49)
Then,
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{X}_{n}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{Y}_{n}\right]\;,
(4.50)
and the submajorization follows from the first part of Lemma 4.13.
∎
Lemma 4.16.
The Fock rearrangement is continuous in the Hilbert-Schmidt norm.
Proof.
Let \hat{X} and \hat{Y} be trace-class operators, with eigenvalues in decreasing order \{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} and \{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} , respectively.
We then have
\left\|\hat{X}^{\downarrow}-\hat{Y}^{\downarrow}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(x_{n}-y_{n})^{2}\leq\left\|\hat{X}-\hat{Y}\right\|_{2}^{2}\;,
(4.51)
where we have used Lemma 4.5.
∎
4.4 Fock-optimal quantum operations
We will prove that any gauge-covariant Gaussian quantum channel satisfies this property:
Definition 4.17 (Fock-optimal quantum operation).
We say that a quantum operation \Phi is Fock-optimal if for any positive trace-class operator \hat{X}
\Phi\left(\hat{X}\right)\prec_{w}\Phi\left(\hat{X}^{\downarrow}\right)\;,
(4.52)
i.e. Fock-rearranging the input always makes the output less noisy, or among all the quantum states with a given spectrum, the passive one generates the least noisy output (see Section 2.7 for the definitions of majorization and weak submajorization).
Remark 4.18.
If \Phi is trace-preserving, weak sub-majorization in (4.52) can be equivalently replaced by majorization.
We can now state the main result of the Chapter:
Theorem 4.19.
Any one-mode gauge-covariant Gaussian quantum channel is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal.
Proof.
See Section 4.5.
∎
Corollary 4.20.
Any linear combination with positive coefficients of gauge-covariant quantum Gaussian channels is Fock-optimal.
Proof.
Follows from Theorem 4.19 and Lemma 4.26.
∎
In the remainder of this Section, we prove some general properties of Fock-optimality that will be needed in the main proof.
Lemma 4.21.
Let \Phi be a passive-preserving quantum operation.
If for any finite-rank projector \hat{P}
\Phi\left(\hat{P}\right)\prec_{w}\Phi\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)\;,
(4.53)
then \Phi is Fock-optimal.
Proof.
Let \hat{X} be a positive trace-class operator as in (4.44), with Fock rearrangement as in (4.46).
Since \Phi is passive-preserving, for any n\in\mathbb{N}
\Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}_{n}\right)\prec_{w}\Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\right)=\Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\right)^{\downarrow}\;.
(4.54)
Then we can apply Lemma 4.15 to
\Phi\left(\hat{X}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}\;\Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}_{n}\right)\prec_{w}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}\;\Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\right)=\Phi\left(\hat{X}^{\downarrow}\right)\;,
(4.55)
and the claim follows.
∎
Lemma 4.22.
A quantum operation \Phi is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal iff
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}\;\Phi\left(\hat{P}\right)\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\;\Phi\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)\right]
(4.56)
for any two finite-rank projectors \hat{Q} and \hat{P} .
Proof.
Let us first suppose that \Phi is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal, and let \hat{P} and \hat{Q} be finite-rank projectors.
Then
\Phi\left(\hat{P}\right)\prec_{w}\Phi\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)=\Phi\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)^{\downarrow}\;,
(4.57)
and (4.56) follows from Lemma 4.13.
Let us now suppose that (4.56) holds for any finite-rank projectors \hat{P} and \hat{Q} .
Choosing \hat{P} passive, we get
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}\;\Phi\left(\hat{P}\right)\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\;\Phi\left(\hat{P}\right)\right]\;,
(4.58)
and from Lemma 4.10 also \Phi\left(\hat{P}\right) is passive, so from Lemma 4.12 \Phi is passive-preserving.
Choosing now a generic \hat{P} , by Lemma 4.13
\Phi\left(\hat{P}\right)\prec_{w}\Phi\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)\;,
(4.59)
and from Lemma 4.21 \Phi is also Fock-optimal.
∎
We can now prove the two fundamental properties of Fock-optimality:
Theorem 4.23.
Let \Phi be a quantum operation with the restriction of its Hilbert-Schmidt dual \Phi^{\dagger} (see (A.59) in Appendix A) to trace-class operators continuous in the trace norm.
Then, \Phi is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal iff \Phi^{\dagger} is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal.
Proof.
Condition (4.56) can be rewritten as
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi^{\dagger}\left(\hat{Q}\right)\hat{P}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi^{\dagger}\left(\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\right)\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right]\;,
(4.60)
and is therefore symmetric for \Phi and \Phi^{\dagger} .
∎
Theorem 4.24.
Let \Phi_{1} and \Phi_{2} be passive-preserving and Fock-optimal quantum operations with the restriction of \Phi_{2}^{\dagger} to trace-class operators continuous in the trace norm.
Then, their composition \Phi_{2}\circ\Phi_{1} is also passive-preserving and Fock-optimal.
Proof.
Let \hat{P} and \hat{Q} be finite-rank projectors.
Since \Phi_{2} is Fock-optimal and passive-preserving,
\Phi_{2}\left(\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}\right)\right)\prec_{w}\Phi_{2}\left(\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}\right)^{\downarrow}\right)=\Phi_{2}\left(\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}\right)^{\downarrow}\right)^{\downarrow}\;,
(4.61)
and by Lemma 4.13
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}\;\Phi_{2}\left(\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}\right)\right)\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\;\Phi_{2}\left(\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}\right)^{\downarrow}\right)\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\left(\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\right)\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}\right)^{\downarrow}\right]\;.
(4.62)
Since \Phi_{1} is Fock-optimal and passive-preserving,
\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}\right)^{\downarrow}\prec_{w}\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)=\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)^{\downarrow}\;.
(4.63)
From Theorem 4.23 also \Phi_{2}^{\dagger} is passive-preserving, and \Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\left(\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\right) is passive.
Lemma 4.14 implies then
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\left(\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\right)\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}\right)^{\downarrow}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\left(\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\right)\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\;\Phi_{2}\left(\Phi_{1}\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)\right)\right]\;,
(4.64)
and the claim follows from Lemma 4.22 combining (4.64) with (4.62).
∎
Lemma 4.25.
Let \Phi be a quantum operation continuous in the Hilbert-Schmidt norm.
Let us suppose that for any N\in\mathbb{N} its restriction to the span of the first N+1 Fock states is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal, i.e. for any positive operator \hat{X} supported on the span of the first N+1 Fock states
\Phi\left(\hat{X}\right)\prec_{w}\Phi\left(\hat{X}^{\downarrow}\right)=\Phi\left(\hat{X}^{\downarrow}\right)^{\downarrow}\;.
(4.65)
Then, \Phi is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal.
Proof.
Let \hat{P} and \hat{Q} be two generic finite-rank projectors.
Since the restriction of \Phi to the support of \hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow} is Fock-optimal and passive-preserving,
\Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{P}\;\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\right)\prec_{w}\Phi\left(\left(\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{P}\;\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\right)^{\downarrow}\right)=\left(\Phi\left(\left(\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{P}\;\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\right)^{\downarrow}\right)\right)^{\downarrow}\;.
(4.66)
Then, from Lemma 4.13
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}\;\Phi\left(\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{P}\;\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\right)\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\;\Phi\left(\left(\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{P}\;\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\right)^{\downarrow}\right)\right]\;.
(4.67)
From Lemma 4.9,
\left\|\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{P}\;\hat{\Pi}_{N}^{\downarrow}-\hat{P}\right\|_{2}\to 0\qquad\text{for}\;N\to\infty\;,
(4.68)
and since \Phi , the Fock rearrangement (see Lemma 4.16) and the Hilbert-Schmidt product are continuous in the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, we can take the limit N\to\infty in (4.67) and get
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}\;\Phi\left(\hat{P}\right)\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Q}^{\downarrow}\;\Phi\left(\hat{P}^{\downarrow}\right)\right]\;.
(4.69)
The claim now follows from Lemma 4.22.
∎
Lemma 4.26.
Let \Phi_{1} and \Phi_{2} be Fock-optimal and passive-preserving quantum operations.
Then, also \Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2} is Fock-optimal and passive-preserving.
Proof.
Easily follows from Lemma 4.22.
∎
4.5 Proof of the main Theorem
First, we can reduce the problem to the quantum-limited attenuator:
Lemma 4.27.
If the quantum-limited attenuator is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal, the property extends to any gauge-covariant quantum Gaussian channel.
Proof.
From Section A.7.1 of Appendix A, any quantum gauge-covariant Gaussian channel can be obtained composing a quantum-limited attenuator with a quantum-limited amplifier.
Moreover, the Hilbert-Schmidt dual of a quantum-limited amplifier is proportional to a quantum-limited attenuator, and from Lemma 4.23 also the amplifier is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal.
Finally, the claim follows from Theorem 4.24.
∎
By Lemma 4.25, we can restrict to quantum states \hat{\rho} supported on the span of the first N+1 Fock states.
Let now
\hat{\rho}(t)=e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\;,
(4.70)
where \mathcal{L} is the generator of the quantum-limited attenuator defined in (4.9).
From the explicit representation (4.5), it is easy to see that \hat{\rho}(t) remains supported on the span of the first N+1 Fock states for any t\geq 0 .
In finite dimension, the quantum states with nondegenerate spectrum are dense in the set of all quantum states.
Besides, the spectrum is a continuous function of the operator, and any linear map is continuous.
Then, without loss of generality we can suppose that \hat{\rho} has nondegenerate spectrum.
Let
p(t)=\left(p_{0}(t),\ldots,p_{N}(t)\right)
(4.71)
be the vectors of the eigenvalues of \hat{\rho}(t) in decreasing order, and let
s_{n}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}(t)\;,\qquad n=0,\ldots,\,N\;,
(4.72)
their partial sums, that we similarly collect into the vector s(t) .
Let instead
p_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)=\langle n|e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}^{\downarrow}\right)|n\rangle\;,\qquad n=0,\,\ldots,\,N
(4.73)
be the eigenvalues of e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}^{\downarrow}\right) (recall that it is diagonal in the Fock basis for any t\geq 0 ), and
s_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}^{\downarrow}(t)\;,\qquad n=0,\,\ldots,\,N\;,
(4.74)
their partial sums.
We notice that p(0)=p^{\downarrow}(0) and then s(0)=s^{\downarrow}(0) .
Combining (4.73) with the expression for the Lindbladian (4.9), with the help of (4.2) it is easy to see that the eigenvalues p_{n}^{\downarrow}(t) satisfy
\frac{d}{dt}p_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)=\left(n+1\right)p_{n+1}^{\downarrow}(t)-n\,p_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)\;,
(4.75)
implying
\frac{d}{dt}s_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)=(n+1)\left(s^{\downarrow}_{n+1}(t)-s^{\downarrow}_{n}(t)\right)
(4.76)
for their partial sums.
The proof of Theorem 4.19 is a consequence of:
Lemma 4.28.
The spectrum of \hat{\rho}(t) can be degenerate at most in isolated points.
Lemma 4.29.
s(t) is continuous in t , and for any t\geq 0 such that \hat{\rho}(t) has nondegenerate spectrum it satisfies
\frac{d}{dt}s_{n}(t)\leq(n+1)(s_{n+1}(t)-s_{n}(t))\;,\qquad n=0,\,\ldots,\,N-1\;.
(4.77)
Lemma 4.30.
If s(t) is continuous in t and satisfies (4.77), then
s_{n}(t)\leq s_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)
(4.78)
for any t\geq 0 and n=0,\,\ldots,\,N .
Lemma 4.30 implies that the quantum-limited attenuator is passive-preserving.
Indeed, let us choose \hat{\rho} passive.
Since e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}\right) is diagonal in the Fock basis, s_{n}^{\downarrow}(t) is the sum of the eigenvalues corresponding to the first n+1 Fock states |0\rangle,\;\ldots,\;|n\rangle .
Since s_{n}(t) is the sum of the n+1 greatest eigenvalues, s_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)\leq s_{n}(t) .
However, Lemma 4.30 implies s_{n}(t)=s_{n}^{\downarrow}(t) for n=0,\,\ldots,\,N .
Thus p_{n}(t)=p_{n}^{\downarrow}(t) , so the operator e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}\right) is passive for any t , and the channel e^{t\mathcal{L}} is passive-preserving.
Then from the definition of majorization and Lemma 4.30 again,
e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}\right)\prec_{w}e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}^{\downarrow}\right)
(4.79)
for any \hat{\rho} , and the quantum-limited attenuator is also Fock-optimal.
4.5.1 Proof of Lemma 4.28
The matrix elements of the operator e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}\right) are analytic functions of t .
The spectrum of \hat{\rho}(t) is degenerate iff the function
\phi(t)=\prod_{i\neq j}\left(p_{i}(t)-p_{j}(t)\right)
(4.80)
vanishes.
This function is a symmetric polynomial in the eigenvalues of \hat{\rho}(t)=e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}\right) .
Then, for the Fundamental Theorem of Symmetric Polynomials (see e.g Theorem 3 in Chapter 7 of [98]), \phi(t) can be written as a polynomial in the elementary symmetric polynomials in the eigenvalues of \hat{\rho}(t) .
However, these polynomials coincide with the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial of \hat{\rho}(t) , that are in turn polynomials in its matrix elements.
It follows that \phi(t) can be written as a polynomial in the matrix elements of the operator \hat{\rho}(t) .
Since each of these matrix element is an analytic function of t , also \phi(t) is analytic.
Since by hypothesis the spectrum of \hat{\rho}(0) is nondegenerate, \phi cannot be identically zero, and its zeroes are isolated points.
4.5.2 Proof of Lemma 4.29
The matrix elements of the operator e^{t\mathcal{L}}\left(\hat{\rho}\right) are analytic (and hence continuous and differentiable) functions of t .
Then for Weyl’s Perturbation Theorem p(t) is continuous in t , and also s(t) is continuous (see e.g. Corollary III.2.6 and the discussion at the beginning of Chapter VI of [95]).
Let \hat{\rho}(t_{0}) have nondegenerate spectrum.
Then, \hat{\rho}(t) has nondegenerate spectrum for any t in a suitable neighbourhood of t_{0} .
In this neighbourhood, we can diagonalize \hat{\rho}(t) with
\hat{\rho}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{N}p_{n}(t)|\psi_{n}(t)\rangle\langle\psi_{n}(t)|\;,
(4.81)
where the eigenvalues in decreasing order p_{n}(t) are differentiable functions of t (see Theorem 6.3.12 of [99]),
and
\frac{d}{dt}p_{n}(t)=\langle\psi_{n}(t)|\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\rho}(t)\right)|\psi_{n}(t)\rangle\;.
(4.82)
We then have
\frac{d}{dt}s_{n}(t)=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\rho}(t)\right)\right]\;,
(4.83)
where
\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\psi_{i}(t)\rangle\langle\psi_{i}(t)|\;.
(4.84)
We can write
\hat{\rho}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{N}d_{n}(t)\;\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;,
(4.85)
where
d_{n}(t)=p_{n}(t)-p_{n+1}(t)\geq 0\;,
(4.86)
so that
\frac{d}{dt}s_{n}(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}d_{k}(t)\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\right)\right]\;.
(4.87)
With the explicit expression (4.9) for \mathcal{L} , it is easy to prove that
\sum_{k=0}^{N}d_{k}(t)\;\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\right)\right]=(n+1)(s_{n+1}(t)-s_{n}(t))\;,
(4.88)
so it would be sufficient to show that
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\right)\right]\overset{?}{\leq}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\right)\right]\;.
(4.89)
We write explicitly the left-hand side of (4.89):
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\hat{a}\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\right]\;,
(4.90)
where we have used that \hat{\Pi}_{n}(t) and \hat{\Pi}_{k}(t) commute.
•
Let us suppose n\geq k .
Then
\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)=\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\;.
(4.91)
Using that \hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\leq\hat{\mathbb{I}} in the first term of (4.90), we get
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\hat{a}\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\right]\leq 0\;.
(4.92)
On the other hand, since the support of \hat{a}\,\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\,\hat{a}^{\dagger} is contained in the support of \hat{\Pi}_{k-1}^{\downarrow} , and hence in the one of \hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow} , we have also
\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}=\hat{a}\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}\;,
(4.93)
so that
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\right]=0\;.
(4.94)
•
Let us now suppose that k\geq n+1 .
Then
\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)=\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;.
(4.95)
Using that \hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\leq\hat{\mathbb{I}} in the first term of (4.90), together with the commutation relation (A.6), we get
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\hat{a}\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\hat{\Pi}_{n}(t)\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}(t)\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\right]\leq n+1\;.
(4.96)
On the other hand, since the support of \hat{a}^{\dagger}\,\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\,\hat{a} is contained in the support of \hat{\Pi}_{n+1}^{\downarrow} and hence in the one of \hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow} , we have also
\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}\;\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}=\hat{a}^{\dagger}\;\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}\;,
(4.97)
so that
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{\Pi}_{k}^{\downarrow}\;\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\right]=n+1\;.
(4.98)
4.5.3 Proof of Lemma 4.30
Since the quantum-limited attenuator is trace-preserving, we have
s_{N}(t)=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\rho}(t)\right]=1=s_{N}^{\downarrow}(t)\;.
(4.99)
We will use induction on n in the reverse order: let us suppose to have proved
s_{n+1}(t)\leq s_{n+1}^{\downarrow}(t)\;.
(4.100)
We then have from (4.77)
\frac{d}{dt}s_{n}(t)\leq(n+1)\left(s_{n+1}^{\downarrow}(t)-s_{n}(t)\right)\;,
(4.101)
while
\frac{d}{dt}s_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)=(n+1)\left(s_{n+1}^{\downarrow}(t)-s_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)\right)\;.
(4.102)
Defining
f_{n}(t)=s_{n}^{\downarrow}(t)-s_{n}(t)\;,
(4.103)
we have f_{n}(0)=0 , and
\frac{d}{dt}f_{n}(t)\geq-(n+1)f_{n}(t)\;.
(4.104)
This can be rewritten as
e^{-(n+1)t}\;\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{(n+1)t}\;f_{n}(t)\right)\geq 0\;,
(4.105)
and implies
f_{n}(t)\geq 0\;.
(4.106)
4.6 Generic one-mode Gaussian channels
In this Section we extend Theorem 4.19 to any one-mode quantum Gaussian channel.
Definition 4.31.
We say that two quantum channels \Phi and \Psi are equivalent if there are a unitary operator \hat{U} and a unitary or anti-unitary \hat{V} such that
\Psi\left(\hat{X}\right)=\hat{V}\;\Phi\left(\hat{U}\;\hat{X}\;\hat{U}^{\dagger}\right)\;\hat{V}^{\dagger}
(4.107)
for any trace-class operator \hat{X} .
Clearly, a channel equivalent to a Fock-optimal channel is also Fock-optimal with a suitable redefinition of the Fock rearrangement:
Lemma 4.32.
Let \Phi be a Fock-optimal quantum channel, and \Psi be as in (4.107).
Then, for any positive trace-class operator \hat{X} ,
\Psi\left(\hat{X}\right)\prec_{w}\Psi\left(\hat{U}^{\dagger}\left(\hat{U}\;\hat{X}\;\hat{U}^{\dagger}\right)^{\downarrow}\hat{U}\right)\;.
(4.108)
The problem of analyzing any Gaussian quantum channel from the point of view of majorization is then reduced to the equivalence classes.
4.6.1 Quadratures and squeezing
In this Section, differently from the rest of the Chapter, \hat{Q} and \hat{P} will denote the quadratures (2.1), and not generic projectors.
We can define a continuous basis of not normalizable vectors \left\{|q\rangle\right\}_{q\in\mathbb{R}} with
\displaystyle\hat{Q}|q\rangle
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle q|q\rangle\;,
(4.109)
\displaystyle\langle q|q^{\prime}\rangle
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\delta(q-q^{\prime})\;,
(4.110)
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|q\rangle\langle q|\;dq
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{I}}\;,
(4.111)
\displaystyle e^{-iq\hat{P}}|q^{\prime}\rangle
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle|q^{\prime}+q\rangle\;,\qquad q,\,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\;.
(4.112)
For any \kappa>0 we define the squeezing unitary operator [60] \hat{S}_{\kappa} with
\hat{S}_{\kappa}|q\rangle=\sqrt{\kappa}\;|\kappa q\rangle
(4.113)
for any q\in\mathbb{R} .
It satisfies also
\hat{S}_{\kappa}^{\dagger}\;\hat{P}\;\hat{S}_{\kappa}=\frac{1}{\kappa}\;\hat{P}\;.
(4.114)
4.6.2 Classification theorem
Then, the following classification theorem holds [86, 2]:
Theorem 4.33.
Any one-mode trace-preserving quantum Gaussian channel is equivalent to one of the following:
1.
a gauge-covariant Gaussian channel, i.e. a channel commuting with the time evolution generated by the photon-number Hamiltonian (4.3) (cases A_{1}) , B_{2}) , C) and D) of [86]);
2.
a measure-reprepare channel \Phi of the form
\Phi\left(\hat{X}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle q|\hat{X}|q\rangle\;e^{-iq\hat{P}}\;\hat{\rho}_{0}\;e^{iq\hat{P}}\;dq
(4.115)
for any trace-class operator \hat{X} , where \rho_{0} is a given Gaussian state (case A_{2}) of [86]);
3.
a random unitary channel \Phi_{\sigma} of the form
\Phi_{\sigma}\left(\hat{X}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-iq\hat{P}}\;\hat{X}\;e^{iq\hat{P}}\;\frac{e^{-\frac{q^{2}}{2\sigma}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma}}\;dq
(4.116)
for any trace-class operator \hat{X} , with \sigma>0 (case B_{1}) of [86]).
From Lemma 4.32, with a suitable redefinition of Fock rearrangement all the channels of the first class are Fock-optimal.
On the contrary, for both the second and the third classes the optimal basis would be an infinitely squeezed version of the Fock basis:
4.6.3 Class 2
We will show that the channel (4.115) does not have optimal inputs.
Let \hat{\omega} be a generic quantum state.
Since \Phi applies a random displacement to the state \hat{\rho}_{0} ,
\Phi\left(\hat{\omega}\right)\prec\hat{\rho}_{0}\;.
(4.117)
Moreover, \Phi\left(\hat{\omega}\right) and \hat{\rho}_{0} cannot have the same spectrum unless the probability distribution \langle q|\hat{\omega}|q\rangle is a Dirac delta, but this is never the case for any quantum state \hat{\omega} , so the majorization in (4.117) is always strict.
Besides, in the limit of infinite squeezing the output tends to \hat{\rho}_{0} in trace norm:
\displaystyle\left\|\Phi\left(\hat{S}_{\kappa}\;\hat{\omega}\;\hat{S}_{\kappa}^{\dagger}\right)-\hat{\rho}_{0}\right\|_{1}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle q|\hat{\omega}|q\rangle\left(e^{-i\kappa q\hat{P}}\;\hat{\rho}_{0}\;e^{i\kappa q\hat{P}}-\hat{\rho}_{0}\right)dq\right\|_{1}\leq
(4.118)
\displaystyle\leq
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle q|\hat{\omega}|q\rangle\left\|e^{-i\kappa q\hat{P}}\;\hat{\rho}_{0}\;e^{i\kappa q\hat{P}}-\hat{\rho}_{0}\right\|_{1}dq\;,
and the last integral tends to zero for \kappa\to 0 since the integrand is dominated by the integrable function 2\langle q|\hat{\omega}|q\rangle , and tends to zero pointwise.
It follows that the majorization relation
\Phi\left(\hat{S}_{\kappa}\;\hat{\omega}\;\hat{S}_{\kappa}\right)\prec\Phi\left(\hat{\omega}\right)
(4.119)
will surely not hold for some positive \kappa in a neighbourhood of 0 , and \hat{\omega} is not an optimal input for \Phi .
4.6.4 Class 3
For the channel (4.116), squeezing the input always makes the output strictly less noisy.
Indeed, it is easy to show that for any positive \sigma and \sigma^{\prime}
\Phi_{\sigma}\circ\Phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\Phi_{\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}\;.
(4.120)
Then, for any \kappa>1 and any positive trace-class \hat{X}
\hat{S}_{\kappa}\;\Phi_{\sigma}\left(\hat{X}\right)\;\hat{S}_{\kappa}^{\dagger}=\Phi_{\kappa^{2}\sigma}\left(\hat{S}_{\kappa}\;\hat{X}\;\hat{S}_{\kappa}^{\dagger}\right)=\Phi_{(\kappa^{2}-1)\sigma}\left(\Phi_{\sigma}\left(\hat{S}_{\kappa}\;\hat{X}\;\hat{S}_{\kappa}^{\dagger}\right)\right)\;,
(4.121)
hence, recalling that \Phi applies a random displacement,
\Phi_{\sigma}\left(\hat{X}\right)\prec\Phi_{\sigma}\left(\hat{S}_{\kappa}\;\hat{X}\;\hat{S}_{\kappa}^{\dagger}\right)\;.
(4.122)
4.7 The thinning
The thinning [87] is the map acting on classical probability distributions on the set of natural numbers that is the discrete analogue of the continuous rescaling operation on positive real numbers.
In this Section we show that the thinning coincides with the restriction of the Gaussian quantum-limited attenuator to quantum states diagonal in the Fock basis, and we hence extend Theorem 4.19 to the discrete classical setting.
Definition 4.34 ( \ell^{1} norm).
The \ell^{1} norm of a sequence \{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} is
\|x\|_{1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|x_{n}|\;.
(4.123)
We say that x is summable if \|x\|_{1}<\infty .
Definition 4.35.
A discrete classical channel is a linear positive map on summable sequences that is continuous in the \ell^{1} norm and preserves the sum, i.e. for any summable sequence x
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[\Phi(x)\right]_{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}\;.
(4.124)
The definitions of passive-preserving and Fock-optimal channels can be easily extended to the discrete classical case:
Definition 4.36.
Given a summable sequence of positive numbers \{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} , we denote with x^{\downarrow} its decreasing rearrangement.
Definition 4.37.
We say that a discrete classical channel \Phi is passive-preserving if for any decreasing summable sequence x of positive numbers \Phi(x) is still decreasing.
Definition 4.38.
We say that a discrete classical channel \Phi is Fock-optimal if for any summable sequence x of positive numbers
\Phi(x)\prec\Phi\left(x^{\downarrow}\right)\;.
(4.125)
Let us now introduce the thinning.
Definition 4.39 (Thinning).
Let N be a random variable with values in \mathbb{N} .
The thinning with parameter 0\leq\lambda\leq 1 is defined as
T_{\lambda}(N)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}B_{i}\;,
(4.126)
where the \{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}} are independent Bernoulli variables with parameter \lambda , i.e. each B_{i} is one with probability \lambda , and zero with probability 1-\lambda .
From a physical point of view, the thinning can be understood as follows:
consider a beam-splitter of transmissivity \lambda , where each incoming photon has probability \lambda of being transmitted, and 1-\lambda of being reflected, and suppose that what happens to a photon is independent from what happens to the other ones.
Let N be the random variable associated to the number of incoming photons, and \{p_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} its probability distribution, i.e. p_{n} is the probability that N=n (i.e. that n photons are sent).
Then, T_{\lambda}(p) is the probability distribution of the number of transmitted photons.
It is easy to show that
\left[T_{\lambda}(p)\right]_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}r_{n|k}\;p_{k}\;,
(4.127)
where the transition probabilities r_{n|k} are given by
r_{n|k}=\binom{k}{n}\lambda^{n}(1-\lambda)^{k-n}\;,
(4.128)
and vanish for k<n .
The map (4.127) can be uniquely extended by linearity to the set of summable sequences:
\left[T_{\lambda}(x)\right]_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}r_{n|k}\;x_{k}\;,\qquad\|x\|_{1}<\infty\;.
(4.129)
Proposition 4.40.
The map T_{\lambda} defined in (4.129) is continuous in the \ell^{1} norm and sum-preserving.
Proof.
For any summable sequence x we have
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|T_{\lambda}(x)\right|_{n}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}r_{n|k}\;|x_{k}|=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}|x_{k}|\;,
(4.130)
where we have used that for any k\in\mathbb{N}
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{n|k}=1\;.
(4.131)
Then, T_{\lambda} is continuous in the \ell^{1} norm.
An analogous proof shows that T_{\lambda} is sum-preserving.
∎
Theorem 4.41.
Let \Phi_{\lambda} and T_{\lambda} be the quantum-limited attenuator and the thinning of parameter 0\leq\lambda\leq 1 , respectively.
Then for any summable sequence x
\Phi_{\lambda}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}\;|n\rangle\langle n|\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[T_{\lambda}(x)\right]_{n}\;|n\rangle\langle n|\;.
(4.132)
Proof.
Easily follows from the representation (4.5), (4.127) and (4.128).
∎
As easy consequence of Theorem 4.41 and Theorem 4.19, we have
Theorem 4.42.
The thinning is passive-preserving and Fock-optimal.
4.8 Conclusion
We have proved that for any one-mode gauge-covariant bosonic Gaussian channel, the output generated by any state diagonal in the Fock basis and with decreasing eigenvalues majorizes the output generated by any other input state with the same spectrum.
Then, the input state with a given entropy minimizing the output entropy is certainly diagonal in the Fock basis and has decreasing eigenvalues.
The non-commutative quantum constrained minimum output entropy conjecture 2.6 is hence reduced to a problem in classical discrete probability, that we will solve in Chapter 5.
Exploiting unitary equivalence we also extend our results to one-mode trace-preserving bosonic Gaussian channel which are not gauge-covariant, with the notable exceptions of those special maps admitting normal forms A_{2}) and B_{1}) [86] for which we show that no general majorization ordering is possible.
Chapter 5 Gaussian states minimize the output entropy of the attenuator
In this Chapter we exploit the majorization result of Chapter 4 to prove that Gaussian thermal input states minimize the output entropy of the one-mode Gaussian quantum-limited attenuator for fixed input entropy.
The Chapter is based on
[46]
G. De Palma, D. Trevisan, and V. Giovannetti, “Gaussian states minimize the output entropy of the one-mode quantum
attenuator,” IEEE Transactions on Information Theory, vol. 63, no. 1,
pp. 728–737, 2017.
http://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/7707386
5.1 Introduction
Most communication schemes encode the information into pulses of electromagnetic radiation, that is transmitted through metal wires, optical fibers or free space, and is unavoidably affected by signal attenuation.
The maximum achievable communication rate of a channel depends on the minimum noise achievable at its output.
A continuous classical signal can be modeled by a real random variable X .
Signal attenuation corresponds to a rescaling X\mapsto\sqrt{\lambda}\,X , where 0\leq\lambda\leq 1 is the attenuation coefficient (the power of the signal is proportional to X^{2} and gets rescaled by \lambda ).
The noise of a real random variable is quantified by its Shannon differential entropy H [12].
The Shannon entropy of the rescaled signal is a simple function of the entropy of the original signal [12]:
H\left(\sqrt{\lambda}\;X\right)=H\left(X\right)+\ln\sqrt{\lambda}\;.
(5.1)
This property is ubiquitous in classical information theory.
For example, it lies at the basis of the proof of the Entropy Power Inequality [29, 30, 31, 32, 33, 34, 12] (see also Section 3.2 and Equation (3.11)).
In the quantum regime the role of the classical Shannon entropy is played by the von Neumann entropy [3, 2] and signal attenuation is modeled by the Gaussian quantum-limited attenuator (see [13, 8, 2, 9, 11] and Section 2.3.1).
A striking consequence of the quantization of the energy is that the output entropy of the quantum-limited attenuator is not a function of the input entropy alone.
