A passive state is diagonal in the Hamiltonian eigenbasis and its eigenvalues decrease as the energy increases.
We have proved that for any one-mode gauge-covariant quantum Gaussian channel, the output generated by a passive state majorizes (i.e. it is less noisy than) the output generated by any other state with the same spectrum, and in particular it has a lower entropy.
Then, the solution to the constrained minimum output entropy problem has certainly to be found among passive states.
We have exploited this result in Chapter 5.
Here we have proved that Gaussian thermal input states minimize the output entropy of the one-mode Gaussian quantum attenuator for fixed input entropy, i.e. conjecture 2.6 for this channel.
The proof is based on the isoperimetric inequality (5.14), whose multimode generalization implies conjecture 2.6 for the multimode attenuator.
The same ideas can be useful in any other entropic optimization problem involving quantum Gaussian channels.
The quantum capacity [3, 2] of a channel is the maximum number of qubits that can be faithfully sent per channel use.
The private capacity [3, 2] is the maximum number of bits per channel use that can be sent and certified not to have been read by any eavesdropper.
So far, both the quantum and the private capacity of quantum Gaussian channels are known only in the degradable case [2].
Our ideas can be useful to determine them in the general case.
In Chapter 6 we have extended the majorization results of Chapter 4 to a large class of lossy quantum channels, resulting from a weak interaction of a small quantum system with a large bath in its ground state.
In Chapter 7 we have considered a particular model of quantum Gaussian channel that implements memory effects, and we have explicitly determined its classical information capacity.
In Chapter 8 we have explored the set of linear trace-preserving not necessarily positive maps preserving the set of Gaussian states.
For one mode, we have proved that any non positive map of this kind is built from the so-called phase-space dilatation.
These maps can be used as tests for certifying that a given quantum state does not belong to the convex hull of Gaussian states, in the same way as positive but not completely positive maps are used as tests for entanglement.
Phase-space dilatations are then proven to be the only relevant test of this kind.
In Chapter 9 we have proved that requiring thermalization of a quantum system in contact with a heat bath for any initial uncorrelated state with a well-defined temperature implies the Eigenstate Thermalization Hypothesis for the system-bath Hamiltonian.
Then, the ETH constitutes the unique criterion to decide whether a given system-bath dynamics always leads to thermalization.
Finally, in Chapter 10 we have proved that any measurement able to distinguish a coherent superposition of two wavepackets of a massive or charged particle from the corresponding incoherent statistical mixture must require a minimum time.
In the case of an electric charge, the bound can be ascribed to the entanglement with the quantum electromagnetic radiation that is unavoidably emitted during the measurement.
Then, in the case of a mass the bound provides an indirect evidence for the existence of quantum gravitational radiation and in general for the necessity of quantizing gravity.
Appendix A Gaussian quantum systems
In this Appendix we provide some technical details on Gaussian quantum information.
In particular, we introduce Gaussian quantum systems in Section A.1, and the method of characteristic functions in Section A.2.
Section A.3 defines the displacement operators, while Section A.4 defines the first and second moment of a quantum state.
Section A.5 introduces quantum Gaussian states, and Section A.6 introduces the method of the Husimi function.
Gaussian quantum channels are defined in Section A.7.
Finally, Section A.8 proves the Entropy Photon-number Inequality (3.106) for Gaussian input states.
A.1 Quadratures and Hilbert space
We consider an n -mode bosonic quantum system with Hilbert space \mathcal{H} and quadrature operators \hat{Q}^{i} and \hat{P}^{i} , i=1,\ldots,\,n , satisfying the canonical commutation relations
\left[\hat{Q}^{i},\;\hat{P}^{j}\right]=i\,\delta^{ij}\;.
(A.1)
As usual, we can define the ladder operators
\hat{a}^{i}=\frac{\hat{Q}^{i}+i\hat{P}^{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\;,
(A.2)
satisfying the commutation relations
\left[\hat{a}^{i},\;\hat{a}^{j{\dagger}}\right]=\delta^{ij}\;.
(A.3)
The number operator is defined as
\hat{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{a}^{i{\dagger}}\hat{a}^{i}\;,
(A.4)
and it counts the number of excitations.
We can put all the quadratures together in the column vector
\hat{\mathbf{R}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{R}^{1}\\
\vdots\\
\hat{R}^{2n}\\
\end{array}\right):=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{Q}^{1}\\
\hat{P}^{1}\\
\vdots\\
\hat{Q}^{n}\\
\hat{P}^{n}\end{array}\right)\;.
(A.5)
The commutation relations (A.1) become
\left[\hat{R}^{i},\;\hat{R}^{j}\right]=i\,\Delta^{ij}\;,
(A.6)
where \Delta is the symplectic form associated with the antisymmetric matrix
\Delta=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\\
\end{array}\right)\;.
(A.7)
An even-dimensional vector space equipped with an nondegenerate antisymmetric bilinear form is called symplectic space, and the bilinear form is called its symplectic form.
It is possible to show [2] that we can always choose a basis such that the matrix associated with the symplectic form has the form (A.7).
A matrix S preserving the symplectic form \Delta , i.e. such that
S\;\Delta\;S^{T}=\Delta\;,
(A.8)
is called symplectic matrix.
The symplectic condition (A.8) simplifies in the case of one
mode. Indeed for any 2\times 2 matrix M we have
M\Delta M^{T}=\Delta\det M\;,
(A.9)
therefore a 2\times 2 matrix S is symplectic iff
\det S=1\;.
(A.10)
A.2 Characteristic and Wigner functions
Let \mathfrak{H} be the set of the Hilbert-Schmidt operators on \mathcal{H} , i.e. the operators with finite Hilbert-Schmidt norm:
\left\|\hat{X}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{X}^{\dagger}\,\hat{X}\right)<\infty\;.
(A.11)
Given an operator \hat{X}\in\mathfrak{H} we define its characteristic function as
\chi_{\hat{X}}(\mathbf{k}):=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{X}\,e^{i\,\mathbf{k}\,\hat{\mathbf{R}}}\right)\;,\qquad\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\;,
(A.12)
where
\mathbf{k}=\left(k_{1},\;\ldots,\;k_{2n}\right)
(A.13)
is a row vector.
It is possible to prove [2, 9] that \hat{X} can be reconstructed with
\hat{X}=\int\chi_{\hat{X}}(\mathbf{k})\;e^{-i\,\mathbf{k}\,\hat{\mathbf{R}}}\;\frac{d^{2n}k}{(2\pi)^{n}}\;,
(A.14)
and that the characteristic function provides an isometry between \mathfrak{H} and L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2n}\right) (see e.g. Theorem 5.3.3 of [93]):
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{X}^{\dagger}\,\hat{Y}\right)=\int{\chi_{\hat{X}}(\mathbf{k})}^{*}\;\chi_{\hat{Y}}(\mathbf{k})\;\frac{d^{2n}k}{(2\pi)^{n}}\;.
(A.15)
Eq. (A.15) is called noncommutative Parceval’s formula.
We define the Wigner function of \hat{X}\in\mathfrak{H} as the Fourier transform of the characteristic function:
W_{\hat{X}}(\mathbf{x}):=\int\chi_{\hat{X}}(\mathbf{k})\;e^{-i\,\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{x}}\;\frac{d^{2n}k}{(2\pi)^{2n}}\;,
(A.16)
where
\mathbf{x}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x^{1}\\
\vdots\\
x^{2n}\\
\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
(A.17)
is a column vector.
A.3 Displacement operators
We define the displacement operators with
\hat{D}(\mathbf{x}):=e^{i\;\mathbf{x}^{T}\;\Delta^{-1}\;\hat{\mathbf{R}}}\;,
(A.18)
acting on the quadratures as
{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}\;\hat{\mathbf{R}}\;\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})=\hat{\mathbf{R}}+\mathbf{x}\;.
(A.19)
Using (A.19), the displacements act on the characteristic function as
\chi_{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{X}\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{x})}^{\dagger}}(\mathbf{k})=e^{i\;\mathbf{k}\;\mathbf{x}}\;\chi_{\hat{X}}(\mathbf{k})\;.
(A.20)
A.4 Moments and symplectic eigenvalues
Given a state \hat{\rho}\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{H}) and an observable \hat{A} , we define its expectation value
\left\langle\hat{A}\right\rangle:=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{\rho}\,\hat{A}\right)\;.
(A.21)
We can now define the first moments of \hat{\rho} as the expectation values of the quadratures
\mathbf{r}:=\left\langle\hat{\mathbf{R}}\right\rangle\;,
(A.22)
and the covariance matrix as
\sigma^{ij}:=\left\langle\left\{\hat{R}^{i}-r^{i},\;\hat{R}^{j}-r^{j}\right\}\right\rangle\;,
(A.23)
where \left\{\cdot,\cdot\right\} stands for the anti-commutator.
It is possible to prove [2] that the eigenvalues of the matrix \sigma\Delta^{-1} are pure imaginary, and since the matrix is real, they come in couples of complex conjugates.
Their absolute values \nu_{k},\;k=1,\ldots,n are called the symplectic eigenvalues of \sigma , and satisfy
\det\sigma=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\nu_{k}^{2}\;.
(A.24)
According to the Williamson theorem [227], for any strictly positive \sigma there exists a symplectic matrix S such that
S\;\sigma\;S^{T}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\nu_{k}\;\mathbb{I}_{2}\;,
(A.25)
where the \nu_{k} are its symplectic eigenvalues.
The positivity of \hat{\rho} together with the commutation relations (A.6) imply for \sigma the Robertson-Heisenberg uncertainty relation
\sigma\geq\pm i\Delta\;.
(A.26)
Condition (A.26) is equivalent to imposing all the symplectic eigenvalues of \sigma to be greater or equal than one.
A symmetric positive definite 2\times 2 -matrix \sigma has a single symplectic eigenvalue, given by
\nu^{2}=\det\sigma\;;
(A.27)
and it is the covariance matrix of a quantum
state iff
\det\sigma\geq 1\;.
(A.28)
The first moment \mathbf{r} and the covariance matrix \sigma can both be computed from the characteristic function with
\displaystyle\mathbf{r}
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-i\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{k}}\ln\chi(\mathbf{k})\right|_{\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0}}
(A.29)
\displaystyle\sigma
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{k}}\;\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{k}}\right)^{T}\ln\chi(\mathbf{k})\right|_{\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0}}\;,
(A.30)
where \frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{k}} is the column vector of the derivatives
\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{k}}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{2n}}\\
\end{array}\right)\;.
(A.31)
A.5 Gaussian states
Given a symmetric matrix \sigma\geq\pm i\Delta and a column vector \mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n} , the Gaussian state \hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma,\,\mathbf{r}) with covariance matrix \sigma and first moment \mathbf{r} is the state
\hat{\rho}_{G}=e^{-\left(\hat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{r}\right)^{T}J\left(\hat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{r}\right)}\left/\mathrm{Tr}\;e^{-\left(\hat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{r}\right)^{T}J\left(\hat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{r}\right)}\right.\;,
(A.32)
where J is the positive real matrix such that
\sigma=\Delta\cot\left(J\;\Delta\right)\;.
(A.33)
It has characteristic function
\chi(\mathbf{k})=e^{-\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,\sigma\,\mathbf{k}^{T}+i\,\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{r}}
(A.34)
and Wigner function
W(\mathbf{x})=\frac{e^{-\left(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{r}\right)^{T}\sigma^{-1}\left(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{r}\right)}}{\sqrt{\det\left(\pi\,\sigma\right)}}\;.
(A.35)
For \sigma=\mathbb{I}_{2n} we obtain the family of coherent states \hat{\rho}_{G}(\mathbf{r},\,\mathbb{I}_{2n}),\;\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n} .
For simplicity, we call \hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma) the centered state \hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma,\,\mathbf{0}) .
A.5.1 Entropy of Gaussian states
The von Neumann entropy S\left[\hat{\rho}\right]=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\rho}\ln\hat{\rho}\right] of the Gaussian state \hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma) with covariance matrix \sigma is given by
S\left(\hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma)\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}h\left(\nu_{k}\right)\;,
(A.36)
where the \nu_{k} are the symplectic eigenvalues of \sigma and
h(\nu)=\frac{\nu+1}{2}\ln\frac{\nu+1}{2}-\frac{\nu-1}{2}\ln\frac{\nu-1}{2}\;.
(A.37)
For thermal states with covariance matrix proportional to the identity, i.e. \sigma=\nu\mathbb{I}_{2n} , it can be useful to express the entropy in terms of the mean photon number per mode
N=\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{N}\;\hat{\rho}\right]=\frac{\nu-1}{2}\;.
(A.38)
For this purpose, it is sufficient to replace the function h(\nu) in (A.36) with
g(N):=h\left(2N+1\right)=(N+1)\ln(N+1)-N\ln N\;.
(A.39)
In the proof of the quantum Entropy Power Inequality in Chapter 3 we have used the asymptotic scaling of the entropy for Gaussian states with large covariance in Eqs. (3.99) and (3.103).
Here we prove this result.
For \nu\to\infty , the function h is almost a logarithm:
h(\nu)=\ln\frac{\nu}{2}+1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\right)\;,
(A.40)
so the entropy of \hat{\rho}_{G}(t\sigma) for t\to\infty is
S\left(\hat{\rho}_{G}(t\sigma)\right)=\ln\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{t\,\nu_{k}}{2}\;+n+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{t^{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\ln\det\left(\frac{e\,t\,\sigma}{2}\right)\;+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{t^{2}}\right)\;,
(A.41)
where we have used (A.24).
A.6 Husimi function
Choose a covariance matrix \gamma\geq\pm i\Delta .
The generalized Husimi function Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x}) of a state \hat{\rho} [17] is its overlap with the Gaussian state \hat{\rho}_{G}(\gamma,\,\mathbf{x}) :
Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{\rho}\;\hat{\rho}_{G}(\gamma,\,\mathbf{x})\right)\;.
(A.42)
We notice that for \gamma=\mathbb{I}_{2n} we recover the usual Husimi function of [60] based on coherent states.
By construction, Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x}) is continuous in \mathbf{x} and positive:
Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x})\geq 0\;.
(A.43)
In terms of the characteristic function of \hat{\rho} , (A.42) reads
Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x})=\int e^{-\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,\gamma\,\mathbf{k}^{T}-i\,\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{x}}\;\chi_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{k})\;\frac{d^{2n}k}{(2\pi)^{2n}}\;,
(A.44)
where we have used (A.34) and (A.15).
The Fourier transform of Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x})
\widetilde{Q}_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{k})=\int Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x})\;e^{i\,\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{x}}\;d^{2n}x
(A.45)
is then given by
\widetilde{Q}_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{k})=e^{-\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,\gamma\,\mathbf{k}^{T}}\;\chi_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{k})\;.
(A.46)
Computing (A.46) in \mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0} and remembering that
\chi_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{0})=\mathrm{Tr}\,\hat{\rho}=1
(A.47)
for any normalized state \hat{\rho} , we can see that the generalized Husimi function Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x}) is a probability distribution:
\int Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x})\;d^{2n}x=1\;.
(A.48)
Besides, it is possible to show [59] that putting \gamma=0 in (A.46) we formally recover the Wigner function.
Now we can express \hat{\rho} in terms of Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x}) : putting together (A.14) and (A.46) we get
\hat{\rho}=\int\widetilde{Q}_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{k})\;e^{\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,\gamma\,\mathbf{k}^{T}}\;e^{-i\,\mathbf{k}\,\hat{\mathbf{R}}}\;\frac{d^{2n}k}{(2\pi)^{n}}=\int Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x})\left(\int e^{\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,\gamma\,\mathbf{k}^{T}+i\,\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{x}}\;e^{-i\,\mathbf{k}\,\hat{\mathbf{R}}}\;\frac{d^{2n}k}{(2\pi)^{n}}\right)d^{2n}x\,.
(A.49)
Comparing with (A.14) the integral in parenthesis, it looks like the Gaussian “state” with covariance matrix -\gamma displaced by \mathbf{x} . Of course, this is not a well-defined state, and it makes sense only if integrated against smooth functions as Q_{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}) . However, if we formally define
\hat{\rho}_{G}(-\gamma,\,\mathbf{x}):=\int e^{\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,\gamma\,\mathbf{k}^{T}+i\,\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{x}}\;e^{-i\,\mathbf{k}\,\hat{\mathbf{R}}}\;\frac{d^{2n}k}{(2\pi)^{n}}\;,
(A.50)
(A.49) becomes
\hat{\rho}=\int Q_{\hat{\rho}}(\mathbf{x})\;\hat{\rho}_{G}(-\gamma,\,\mathbf{x})\;d^{2n}x\;.
(A.51)
Then the Husimi function of any bounded operator \hat{\rho} uniquely defines \hat{\rho} .
It follows that the linear span of the set of coherent states, and hence of all Gaussian states, is dense in the Hilbert space of
Hilbert-Schmidt operators \mathfrak{H} . Similarly, these linear spans are dense in the Banach space of trace-class
operators \mathfrak{T} .
A.7 Quantum Gaussian channels
Let X and Y be two symplectic spaces, with symplectic forms \Delta_{X} and \Delta_{Y} and associated sets of trace-class operators \mathfrak{T}_{X} and \mathfrak{T}_{Y} , respectively.
Given a matrix
M:X\longrightarrow Y\;,
(A.52)
a covariance matrix \alpha on Y and a column vector \mathbf{y}\in Y , we define the quantum Gaussian channel
\Phi:\mathfrak{T}_{X}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{T}_{Y}
(A.53)
of parameters (M,\;\alpha,\;\mathbf{y}) as the channel that acts on the characteristic function as
\chi_{\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}\right)}(\mathbf{k})=e^{-\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,\alpha\,\mathbf{k}^{T}+i\,\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{y}}\;\chi_{\hat{\rho}}\left(\mathbf{k}\,M\right)\;,
(A.54)
for any row vector \mathbf{k} in Y .
The channel defined in (A.54) is completely positive (see [2]) iff
\alpha\geq\pm i\left(\Delta_{Y}-M\;\Delta_{X}\;M^{T}\right)\;.
(A.55)
\Phi sends Gaussian states in \mathfrak{S}_{X} into Gaussian states in \mathfrak{S}_{Y} :
\Phi\left(\hat{\rho}_{G}(\sigma,\,\mathbf{r})\right)=\hat{\rho}_{G}\left(M\,\sigma\,M^{T}+\alpha,\;M\,\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{y}\right)\;,
(A.56)
and it acts on the moments as
\displaystyle\sigma
\displaystyle\mapsto
\displaystyle M\,\sigma\,M^{T}+\alpha
(A.57)
\displaystyle\mathbf{r}
\displaystyle\mapsto
\displaystyle M\,\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{y}\;.
(A.58)
From the action on the characteristic function (A.56) and Parceval’s formula (A.15), it is easy to prove that any quantum Gaussian channel is continuous in the Hilbert-Schmidt norm (A.11).
The Hilbert-Schmidt dual of a linear map \Phi acting on trace-class operators and continuous in the trace norm is the map \Phi^{\dagger} acting on bounded operators and continuous in the operator norm defined by
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{Y}\;\Phi\left(\hat{X}\right)\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\Phi^{\dagger}\left(\hat{Y}\right)\;\hat{X}\right]
(A.59)
for any trace-class operator \hat{X} and any bounded operator \hat{Y} .
If the matrix M is invertible, with Parceval’s formula (A.15) it is easy to see that the Hilbert-Schmidt adjoint of the Gaussian quantum channel \Phi with parameters \left(M,\;\alpha,\;\mathbf{y}\right) defined in (A.56) acts on the characteristic function as
\chi_{\Phi^{\dagger}\left(\hat{Y}\right)}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\det M}\;e^{-\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{k}\,M^{-1}\,\alpha M^{-T}\,\mathbf{k}^{T}-i\,\mathbf{k}\,M^{-1}\,\mathbf{y}}\;\chi_{\hat{Y}}\left(\mathbf{k}\,M^{-1}\right)\;.
(A.60)
It is possible to prove [2] that any quantum Gaussian channel admits a Gaussian Stinespring dilation, i.e. there exist two symplectic spaces Z and W with
\dim X+\dim Z=\dim Y+\dim W\;,
(A.61)
a Gaussian state \hat{\rho}_{Z} on \mathcal{H}_{Z} and a symplectic matrix
S:X\oplus Z\to Y\oplus W
(A.62)
satisfying
S\left(\Delta_{X}\oplus\Delta_{Z}\right)S^{T}=\Delta_{Y}\oplus\Delta_{W}
(A.63)
such that for any \hat{X}\in\mathfrak{T}_{X}
\Phi\left(\hat{X}\right)=\hat{D}(\mathbf{y})\;\mathrm{Tr}_{W}\left[\hat{U}_{S}\left(\hat{X}\otimes\hat{\rho}_{Z}\right)\hat{U}_{S}^{\dagger}\right]\;{\hat{D}(\mathbf{y})}^{\dagger}\;.
(A.64)
Here \hat{U}_{S} is the isometry
\hat{U}_{S}:\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z}\to\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{W}
(A.65)
that implements the symplectic matrix S , i.e.
\hat{U}_{S}^{\dagger}\left(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{Y}\oplus\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{W}\right)\hat{U}_{S}=S\left(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{X}\oplus\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{Z}\right)\;,
(A.66)
where \hat{\mathbf{R}}_{X} , \hat{\mathbf{R}}_{Y} , \hat{\mathbf{R}}_{Z} and \hat{\mathbf{R}}_{W} are the vectors of the quadrature operators of the four systems.
A.7.1 Quantum-limited attenuator and amplifier
The Gaussian quantum-limited attenuator \mathcal{E}_{\lambda} , 0\leq\lambda\leq 1 defined in Section 2.3.1 can be recovered from (A.54) identifying Y with X and setting \mathbf{y}=0 , M=\sqrt{\lambda}\;\mathbb{I} and \alpha=(1-\lambda)\mathbb{I} [2].
Its action on the characteristic function is then
\chi_{\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}\left(\hat{\rho}\right)}(\mathbf{k})=e^{-\frac{1-\lambda}{4}\left|\mathbf{k}\right|^{2}}\;\chi_{\hat{\rho}}\left(\sqrt{\lambda}\;\mathbf{k}\right)\;.
(A.67)
The Gaussian quantum-limited amplifier \mathcal{A}_{\kappa} , \kappa\geq 1 can be instead recovered with \mathbf{y}=0 , M=\sqrt{\kappa}\;\mathbb{I} and \alpha=(\kappa-1)\mathbb{I} :
\chi_{\mathcal{A}_{\kappa}\left(\hat{\rho}\right)}(\mathbf{k})=e^{-\frac{\kappa-1}{4}\left|\mathbf{k}\right|^{2}}\;\chi_{\hat{\rho}}\left(\sqrt{\kappa}\;\mathbf{k}\right)\;.
(A.68)
Combining (A.60) with (A.67) and (A.68), it is easy to show that the Hilbert-Schmidt dual of the n -mode quantum-limited attenuator of parameter 0<\lambda\leq 1 is 1/\lambda^{n} times the quantum-limited amplifier of parameter \kappa=1/\lambda\geq 1 , and its restriction to trace-class operators is continuous in the trace-norm (see also [94]).
The quantum-limited attenuator and amplifier are the elementary building blocks for gauge-covariant Gaussian channels.
Indeed, it is possible to prove [228, 16, 18, 11] that any gauge-covariant quantum Gaussian channel can be expressed as a quantum-limited amplifier composed with a quantum-limited attenuator.
A.8 EPnI for Gaussian states
In this Section we prove that the Entropy Photon-number Inequality (3.106) holds when the two inputs are Gaussian states.
Let us consider two n -mode Gaussian states with covariance matrices \sigma_{A} and \sigma_{B} , respectively.
Let 0\leq\lambda\leq 1 be the parameter of the beamsplitter.
From Eqs. (3.15) and (3.26), the covariance matrix of the output C is
\sigma_{C}=\lambda\;\sigma_{A}+(1-\lambda)\;\sigma_{B}\;.
(A.69)
The EPnI (3.106) reads
h^{-1}\left(S_{C}/n\right)\geq\lambda\;h^{-1}\left(S_{A}/n\right)+\left(1-\lambda\right)h^{-1}\left(S_{B}/n\right)\;,
(A.70)
where S_{A} , S_{B} and S_{C} are the entropies of the two inputs and the output, respectively, and the function h has been defined in (A.37) ( h^{-1} is an affine function of g^{-1} , hence the inequality (A.70) can be indifferently written in terms of g or of h ).
Let 1\leq a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{n} , 1\leq b_{1}\leq\ldots\leq b_{n} and 1\leq c_{1}\leq\ldots\leq c_{n} be the symplectic eigenvalues of \sigma_{A} , \sigma_{B} and \sigma_{C} , respectively.
Their entropies are given by
S_{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}h\left(x_{i}\right)\;,\qquad X=A,\,B,\,C\;.
(A.71)
From Theorem 1 of [229],
c\prec^{w}\lambda\;a+(1-\lambda)\;b\;.
(A.72)
The symbol \prec^{w} stands for weak supermajorization [68], the partial order relation between vectors with real components that can be defined as
x\prec^{w}y\quad\text{iff}\quad\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(y_{i})\quad\forall\;\text{increasing concave}\;f\;.
(A.73)
Since h is increasing and concave,
S_{C}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}h\left(c_{i}\right)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}h\left(\lambda a_{i}+(1-\lambda)b_{i}\right)\;.
(A.74)
Let us define
S^{X}_{i}=h(x_{i})\;,\qquad X=A,\,B\;.
(A.75)
Using (A.74), the EPnI (A.70) is implied by
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h\left(\lambda\;h^{-1}\left(S_{i}^{A}\right)+(1-\lambda)\;h^{-1}\left(S_{i}^{B}\right)\right)\overset{?}{\geq}h\left(\lambda\;h^{-1}\left(\frac{S_{A}}{n}\right)+(1-\lambda)\;h^{-1}\left(\frac{S_{B}}{n}\right)\right)\;,
(A.76)
that is equivalent to the convexity of the function
F\left(S_{A},S_{B}\right)=h\left(\lambda\;h^{-1}\left(S_{A}\right)+(1-\lambda)\;h^{-1}\left(S_{B}\right)\right)\;,\qquad S_{A},S_{B}\geq 0\;.
(A.77)
For \lambda=1 , F=S_{A} , while for \lambda=0 , F=S_{B} .
We can then restrict to 0<\lambda<1 .
The determinant of the Hessian of F is
\displaystyle\det H_{F}\left(h(a),h(b)\right)=
\displaystyle=\lambda(1-\lambda)\;h^{\prime}(c)\;h^{\prime\prime}(c)\;\frac{h^{\prime\prime}(a)\;h^{\prime\prime}(b)}{{h^{\prime}(a)}^{3}\;{h^{\prime}(b)}^{3}}\left(\frac{h^{\prime}(c)}{h^{\prime\prime}(c)}-\lambda\;\frac{h^{\prime}(a)}{h^{\prime\prime}(a)}-(1-\lambda)\;\frac{h^{\prime}(b)}{h^{\prime\prime}(b)}\right)\;,
(A.78)
where
c=\lambda a+(1-\lambda)b\;.
(A.79)
Since for Lemma A.1 the function h^{\prime}/h^{\prime\prime} is strictly convex, for any a\neq b we have
\frac{h^{\prime}(c)}{h^{\prime\prime}(c)}<\lambda\frac{h^{\prime}(a)}{h^{\prime\prime}(a)}+(1-\lambda)\frac{h^{\prime}(b)}{h^{\prime\prime}(b)}\;,
(A.80)
and hence \det H_{F}>0 .
Then, in each of the two regions S_{A}>S_{B}>0 and S_{B}>S_{A}>0 , H_{F} is either strictly positive or strictly negative.
To determine which is the case, we compute the Laplacian of F for S_{A}=S_{B}=h(a) :
\mathrm{Tr}\;H_{F}\left(h(a),h(a)\right)=-2\lambda(1-\lambda)\;\frac{h^{\prime\prime}(a)}{{h^{\prime}(a)}^{2}}>0\;.
(A.81)
Then, H_{F} cannot be strictly negative in neither of the regions, and F is convex.
A.8.1 Useful lemmata
Lemma A.1.
For any x>1 the function
\phi(x)=\frac{h^{\prime}(x)}{h^{\prime\prime}(x)}
(A.82)
is strictly convex.
Proof.
Putting
x=\frac{1}{\tanh\theta}\;,\qquad\theta>0\;,
(A.83)
we have
\phi^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{1}{\tanh\theta}\right)=2\left(\sinh\theta\;\cosh\theta-\theta\right)>0\;.
(A.84)
∎
A.9 Quantum Fisher information
Let us consider a family of quantum states \left\{\hat{\rho}_{\theta}\right\}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}} .
In Section 3.3.5 we have defined its quantum Fisher information as
J=\left.\frac{d^{2}}{d\theta^{2}}S\left(\hat{\rho}\left\|\hat{\rho}_{\theta}\right.\right)\right|_{\theta=0}=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\rho}\left.\frac{d^{2}}{d\theta^{2}}\ln\hat{\rho}_{\theta}\right|_{\theta=0}\right]\;,
(A.85)
where \hat{\rho}=\hat{\rho}_{0} .
With the help of the identity
-\ln\hat{\rho}_{\theta}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{t+\hat{\rho}_{\theta}}-\frac{1}{t+1}\right)dt\;,
(A.86)
we get
J=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{\hat{\rho}}{\left(t+\hat{\rho}\right)^{2}}\;\dot{\hat{\rho}}\;\frac{1}{t+\hat{\rho}}\;\dot{\hat{\rho}}\right]dt\;,
(A.87)
where \dot{\hat{\rho}}=\left.\frac{d}{d\theta}\hat{\rho}_{\theta}\right|_{\theta=0} .
Diagonalizing \hat{\rho} as
\hat{\rho}=\sum_{i}p_{i}\;|\psi_{i}\rangle\langle\psi_{i}|\;,\quad\langle\psi_{i}|\psi_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}\;,\quad p_{i}\geq 0\;,\quad\sum_{i}p_{i}=1\;,
(A.88)
we can perform the integral and get
J=\sum_{i}\frac{\left|\langle\psi_{i}|\dot{\hat{\rho}}|\psi_{i}\rangle\right|^{2}}{p_{i}}+\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{\ln p_{i}-\ln p_{j}}{p_{i}-p_{j}}\;\left|\langle\psi_{i}|\dot{\hat{\rho}}|\psi_{j}\rangle\right|^{2}\;.
(A.89)
This is the Kubo-Mori [230] norm squared of \dot{\hat{\rho}} .
The quantum Cramér-Rao bound [78, 79, 80] provides the ultimate bound to the variance of any estimator \tilde{\theta} of \theta :
\mathrm{Var}\;\tilde{\theta}\geq\frac{1}{F}\;,
(A.90)
where F is the Bures norm squared [230] of \dot{\hat{\rho}} :
F=\sum_{ij}\frac{2\left|\langle\psi_{i}|\dot{\hat{\rho}}|\psi_{j}\rangle\right|^{2}}{p_{i}+p_{j}}\;,
(A.91)
and it is also called quantum Fisher information.
J and F coincide when \dot{\hat{\rho}} is diagonal in the eigenbasis of \hat{\rho} .
Since for any p_{i},p_{j}\geq 0
\frac{2}{p_{i}+p_{j}}\leq\frac{\ln p_{i}-\ln p_{j}}{p_{i}-p_{j}}\;,
(A.92)
in general
F\leq J\;.
(A.93)
Appendix B Quantum electrodynamics
In this Appendix we recall some properties of quantum electrodynamics and perform some auxiliary computation whose results are needed in Chapter 10.
In particular, in Section B.1 we show that for Bob a measurement of the position is always better than a measurement of the momentum, i.e. it allows to distinguish the force difference \Delta F in a shorter time.
In Section B.2, we prove that the minimum width over which a charge q greater than the Planck charge q_{\mathrm{P}} can be localized is its charge radius q\hbar/(q_{\mathrm{P}}mc)
[226].
In Section B.3, we recall the basics of the quantization of the electromagnetic field (see also [231]).
In Section B.4, we introduce the set of coherent states of the electromagnetic field (see also [60]).
In Section B.5, we show that the quantum state of the electromagnetic field coupled to a classical charged particle following a non-relativistic motion remains undistinguishable from the vacuum.
In Section B.6, we explain in detail why when Alice’s charged particle is in the quantum superposition (10.1), despite it generates a static electric field that depends on its position, it is still not entangled with the field, and the global state is a product with the field part in the vacuum.
In Section B.7, we explain in detail why Alice can measure only the velocity of her particle, and not its canonical momentum, if she is constrained to remain in her laboratory, which has the size of the support of the wavefunction of the particle.
Finally, in Section B.8 we check the validity of the approximation of neglecting the time T_{D} that Bob needs in order to remove the trapping potential.
B.1 Strength of the trap
Let \omega be the frequency of the harmonic trap. The spatial width of its ground state is given by
\Delta X^{2}\simeq\frac{\hbar}{m_{\mathrm{B}}\,\omega}\;.
(B.1)
This ground state is insensible to the force difference \Delta F iff the displacement that it generates is less than \Delta X , i.e.
\frac{\Delta F}{m_{\mathrm{B}}\,\omega^{2}}\lesssim\Delta X\;.
(B.2)
Eliminating \omega with (B.1), the inequality (B.2) becomes hence
\Delta X^{3}\lesssim\frac{\hbar^{2}}{m_{\mathrm{B}}\,\Delta F}\;,
(B.3)
which is the condition we have to enforce to ensure that Bob’s detector is ineffective when switched off. Let us then suppose that, after switching on the detector, Bob tries to distinguish the two states of Alice by a measurement of P : accordingly the momentum spread \Delta P of his initial state must be lower than the displacement in momentum |\delta_{p}|=\Delta F\,t . Recalling that Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle \Delta X\,\Delta P\geq\hbar is saturated by a Gaussian pure state, the minimum time after which Bob can distinguish is
T_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}=\frac{\hbar}{\Delta F\,\Delta X}\;.
(B.4)
On the other hand, from the ratio considered in (10.14), the minimum discrimination time with a measurement of x is
T_{\mathrm{B}}=\sqrt{\frac{m_{\mathrm{B}}\,\Delta X}{\Delta F}}\;.
(B.5)
The reader can check that (B.3) implies T_{\mathrm{B}}\leq T_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime} , i.e. if the trap is strong enough to be insensible to the force difference, for Bob it is always better to measure the position of his particle rather than its momentum.
B.2 Maximum localization of a charge
Let us suppose to use a harmonic trap of frequency \omega to localize the charge. One could think that in principle, with a strong enough trap, the charge can be arbitrarily localized. However, from the Larmor formula [232] we know that a classical particle with charge q following a harmonic motion of frequency \omega and width \Delta X loses into electromagnetic radiation a power
\frac{dE}{dt}\simeq\frac{q^{2}\,\omega^{4}\,\Delta X^{2}}{\epsilon_{0}\,c^{3}}\;.
(B.6)
In the quantum case, the charge radiates until it gets to the ground state of the trap, where it cannot radiate anymore since there are no other states with a lower energy to go. However, if the trap is very strong, its ground state is very localized, and therefore has a great uncertainty in velocity. Since any moving charge generates a magnetic field, this velocity uncertainty generates a large uncertainty in the magnetic field, resulting in a large entanglement between the state of the particle and the state of the field. Qualitatively, this happens when the energy classically radiated in a period becomes greater than \hbar\omega , the energy of the first excited state. Combining (B.6) with (B.1), this happens exactly when the localization \Delta X becomes smaller that the charge radius:
\Delta X\lesssim\frac{q}{q_{\mathrm{P}}}\,\frac{\hbar}{mc}\;.
(B.7)
Then, if we want the reduced state of the particle to remain pure, we can localize it only up to the limit in (B.7).
B.3 Quantization of the electromagnetic field
We denote with \hat{O} an operator in the Schrödinger picture, and with \hat{O}(t) its counterpart in the Heisenberg picture. The two pictures are defined to coincide for t=0 , i.e. \hat{O}(0)=\hat{O} . We recall that in the Heisenberg picture the operators are evolved with the full interacting Hamiltonian.
An Hamiltonian formulation of electrodynamics requires the introduction of the scalar and vector potentials V and \mathbf{A} . It is convenient to Fourier-transform with respect to \mathbf{x} . The potentials are related to the electric and magnetic fields by
\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{k},t)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle-i\mathbf{k}\;\hat{V}(\mathbf{k},t)-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{k},t)
(B.8)
\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{k},t)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle i\mathbf{k}\times\hat{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{k},t)\;.
(B.9)
We choose the Coulomb gauge, in which the divergence of the vector potential is set to zero at the operator level:
\mathbf{k}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{k},t)=0\;.
(B.10)
It is now convenient to define the ladder operators
\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)\equiv\sqrt{\frac{|\mathbf{k}|}{2}}\;\hat{A}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2|\mathbf{k}|}}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{A}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)\;,
(B.11)
satisfying the constraint k_{i}\,\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)=0 as a consequence of (B.10). The definition in (B.11) can be inverted:
\hat{A}^{i}(\mathbf{x},t)=\int\frac{\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)\;e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}+\hat{a}^{i{\dagger}}(\mathbf{k},t)\;e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}}{\sqrt{2|\mathbf{k}|}}\;\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;.
(B.12)
The ladder operators satisfy the equal-time canonical commutation relations
\displaystyle\left[\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t),\;\hat{a}^{j{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q},t)\right]
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\Pi^{ij}(\mathbf{k})\;(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{3}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})
(B.13)
\displaystyle\left[\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t),\;\hat{a}^{j}(\mathbf{q},t)\right]
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\left[\hat{a}^{i{\dagger}}(\mathbf{k},t),\;\hat{a}^{j{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q},t)\right]=0\;,\quad
(B.14)
where \Pi(\mathbf{k}) is the projector onto the subspace orthogonal to \mathbf{k} :
\Pi^{ij}(\mathbf{k})=\delta^{ij}-\frac{k^{i}\,k^{j}}{\mathbf{k}^{2}}\;.
(B.15)
The vacuum state of the field |0\rangle is defined as the state annihilated by all the Schrödinger-picture annihilation operators:
\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})|0\rangle=0\qquad\forall\;\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\;,\quad i=1,\,2,\,3\;,\qquad|0\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{F}\;.
(B.16)
Besides, the \hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k}) together with their hermitian conjugates \hat{a}^{i{\dagger}}(\mathbf{k}) generate the whole observable algebra of \mathcal{H}_{F} .
Maxwell’s equations determine the time evolution of the ladder operators:
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)+i|\mathbf{k}|\,\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)=\frac{i\,\Pi^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}(\mathbf{k})}{\sqrt{2|\mathbf{k}|}}\hat{J}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)\;,
(B.17)
where \hat{\mathbf{J}} is the operator associated to the current density of the quantum system interacting with the electromagnetic field. Eq. (B.17) is easily integrated:
\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)=e^{-i|\mathbf{k}|t}\left(\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})+\frac{i\,\Pi^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}(\mathbf{k})}{\sqrt{2|\mathbf{k}|}}\;\int_{0}^{t}e^{i|\mathbf{k}|t^{\prime}}\hat{J}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t^{\prime})\;dt^{\prime}\right)\;,
(B.18)
where we have imposed the Heisenberg and Schrödinger pictures to coincide at t=0 , i.e. \hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},0)=\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k}) .
In the free case, i.e. when the current vanishes at the operator level ( \hat{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{k},t)=0 ), the relation between the two pictures is given by the free Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{F}\equiv\int|\mathbf{k}|\;\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\;\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})\;\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;,
(B.19)
i.e.
\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)=e^{-i|\mathbf{k}|t}\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})=e^{i\hat{H}_{F}t}\;\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})\;e^{-i\hat{H}_{F}t}\;.
(B.20)
B.4 Coherent states
For any function f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}^{3} subject to the constraint
k_{i}\,f^{i}(\mathbf{k})=0\qquad\forall\;\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\;,
(B.21)
we define the unitary displacement operator
\hat{D}[f]\equiv\exp\left(\int\left(f_{i}(\mathbf{k})\,\hat{a}^{i{\dagger}}(\mathbf{k})-f_{i}^{*}(\mathbf{k})\,\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})\right)\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\right)\;,\qquad\hat{D}^{\dagger}[f]=\hat{D}[-f]\;,
(B.22)
acting on the ladder operators as
\hat{D}^{\dagger}[f]\;\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})\;\hat{D}[f]=\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})+f^{i}(\mathbf{k})\;.
(B.23)
Their composition rule is
\hat{D}[f]\;\hat{D}[g]=\hat{D}[f+g]\;\exp{\left(\frac{1}{2}\int\left(f^{i}(\mathbf{k})\,g_{i}^{*}(\mathbf{k})-f_{i}^{*}(\mathbf{k})\,g^{i}(\mathbf{k})\right)\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\right)}\;.
(B.24)
We can now define the coherent states with a displacement operator acting on the vacuum state of the field:
|f\rangle\equiv\hat{D}[f]|0\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{F}\;,
(B.25)
that are eigenstates of the annihilation operators:
\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})|f\rangle=f^{i}(\mathbf{k})|f\rangle\;.
(B.26)
Their overlap is
\left|\langle f|g\rangle\right|^{2}=\exp\left(-\int\left|f(\mathbf{k})-g(\mathbf{k})\right|^{2}\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\right)\;.
(B.27)
B.5 Radiation emitted by Alice’s particle
We consider a classical charged particle coupled to the quantum electromagnetic field.
Let \mathbf{J} be the classical current density associated to the trajectory of the particle. Looking at the time evolution equation for the ladder operators (B.18), and recalling (B.23) and (B.20), it is easy to show that such evolution is provided by a displacement operator, i.e.
\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k},t)=\hat{D}^{\dagger}[f]\;e^{i\hat{H}_{F}t}\;\hat{a}^{i}(\mathbf{k})\;e^{-i\hat{H}_{F}t}\;\hat{D}[f]\;,
(B.28)
where
f^{i}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{i\,\Pi^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}(\mathbf{k})}{\sqrt{2|\mathbf{k}|}}\;\int_{0}^{t}e^{i|\mathbf{k}|t^{\prime}}J^{i}(\mathbf{k},t^{\prime})\;dt^{\prime}\;.
(B.29)
Since the ladder operators generate the whole observable algebra of \mathcal{H}_{F} , if the fields starts in the vacuum, its time-evolved state is the coherent state e^{-i\hat{H}_{F}t}|f\rangle . Its overlap with the vacuum can be computed with (B.27):
\left|\langle 0|f\rangle\right|^{2}=\exp\left(-\int\left|f(\mathbf{k})\right|^{2}\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\right)\;.
(B.30)
We now consider a point particle carrying charge q that starts in \mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0} at t=0 , and in a time t_{0} is brought to the position \mathbf{x}=\mathbf{d} with a trajectory described by \mathbf{x}(t) . The current density is then
\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{k},t)=q\;\mathbf{v}(t)\;e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}(t)}\;,
(B.31)
where \mathbf{v}(t)\equiv\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{x}(t) is the particle velocity. For wavelengths large with respect to the extension of the motion, i.e. for
|\mathbf{k}|\ll\frac{1}{d}\;,
(B.32)
the phase factor in (B.31) can be discarded, getting
\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{k},t)\simeq q\;\mathbf{v}(t)\;.
(B.33)
We want to look at the state of the field after the particle has reached the new position \mathbf{x}=\mathbf{d} , i.e. for t>t_{0} . Since the velocity \mathbf{v}(t) vanishes for t\leq 0 and t\geq t_{0} , the displacement of (B.29) becomes
f^{i}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{i\,q}{\sqrt{2|\mathbf{k}|}}\;\Pi^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}(\mathbf{k})\;v^{j}(\omega=|\mathbf{k}|)\;,
(B.34)
where
\mathbf{v}(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathbf{v}(t)\;e^{i\omega t}\;dt
(B.35)
is the Fourier transform of the velocity. Putting (B.34) into (B.30), the overlap becomes
\left|\langle 0|f\rangle\right|^{2}=\exp\left(-\frac{q^{2}}{6\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\mathbf{v}(\omega)|^{2}\;\omega\;d\omega\right)\;.
(B.36)
For simplicity we consider a one-dimensional motion, and we put the x axis in the direction of \mathbf{d} . As an example, we take
x(t)=d\;\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\,\frac{t}{t_{0}}\right)\qquad\text{for}\;0\leq t\leq t_{0}\;,
(B.37)
satisfying the conditions
x(0)=0\;,\qquad x(t_{0})=d\;,\qquad v(0)=v(t_{0})=0\;.
(B.38)
The Fourier transform of the velocity is
v(\omega)=e^{\frac{i\omega t_{0}}{2}}\;\frac{d\cos\frac{\omega t_{0}}{2}}{1-\frac{\omega^{2}t_{0}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}}\;,
(B.39)
and the overlap
\left|\langle 0|f\rangle\right|^{2}=\exp\left(-\pi\,\frac{\pi\mathrm{Si}(\pi)-2}{6}\;\frac{q^{2}}{q_{\mathrm{P}}^{2}}\;\frac{d^{2}}{c^{2}\,t_{0}^{2}}\right)\simeq\exp\left(-2\;\frac{q^{2}}{q_{\mathrm{P}}^{2}}\;\frac{d^{2}}{c^{2}\,t_{0}^{2}}\right)\;,
(B.40)
where \mathrm{Si}(x) is the sine integral function
\mathrm{Si}(x)\equiv\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\sin y}{y}dy\;.
(B.41)
Looking at (B.39), the dominant contribution to the integral in (B.36) comes from the region \omega\,t_{0}\lessapprox 1 . The approximation in (B.32) is then valid iff d\ll c\,t_{0} , i.e. if the motion is not relativistic.
The final result (B.40) tells us that for a fixed distance d , no photons are radiated if the motion lasts for at least
t_{0}\gtrsim\sqrt{2}\;\frac{q}{q_{\mathrm{P}}}\;\frac{d}{c}\;.
(B.42)
Then, Alice can always create the coherent superposition used in the thought experiment without entangling her particle with the emitted photons provided she has enough time to do it. Besides, if Alice wants to perform the particular spin-dependent measurement described in the main text, she needs at least a time (B.42) to bring the state |R\rangle back to |L\rangle if she does not want to entangle with the emitted photons.
B.6 Absence of entanglement with the static electric field
The first Maxwell’s equation reads
\mathbf{k}^{2}\hat{V}(\mathbf{k})=\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{k})\;,
(B.43)
and completely determines the electric potential operator \hat{V} in terms of the charge density operator \hat{\rho} :
\hat{V}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathbf{k}^{2}}\,\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{k})\;.
(B.44)
Putting together (B.44) and (B.8), the electric field is given by
\hat{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{k})=-\frac{i\mathbf{k}}{\mathbf{k}^{2}}\;\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{k})-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{k})\;.
(B.45)
Then, the longitudinal (i.e. proportional to \mathbf{k} ) component of the electric field operator is determined by the charge-density operator, and acts on the Hilbert space of the particle alone. Therefore, even if the field is in its vacuum state (B.16), the expectation value of the electric field is the static Coulomb electric field generated by the expectation value of the charge density, and hence depends on the particle wavefunction. This means that the state of the field alone does not contain all the information on the electric field, since its longitudinal component is encoded into the state of the particle.
Seen from a different perspective, the longitudinal component of the electric field is not a dynamical propagating degree of freedom, since it vanishes in absence of external charges and is completely determined by them, so there is no Hilbert space associated to it. The Hilbert space of the field contains only the degrees of freedom associated to the electromagnetic radiation, i.e. the magnetic field and the transverse (orthogonal to \mathbf{k} ) component of the electric field. Then in a product state with the field part in the vacuum, only these components are in the vacuum mode, while there can be a static electric field depending on the state of the particle.
A final remark should be made about the choice of the gauge. Strictly speaking, the concept of particle-field entanglement is a gauge dependent concept and the previous discussion about the absence of entanglement between a static charge and the electromagnetic field is valid only in the Coulomb gauge. However, despite the initial state we consider would look entangled in a different gauge, obviously the estimation of the minimum discrimination time would not change.
B.7 Locality
The wavefunction \psi(\mathbf{x},t) of a particle carrying electric charge q coupled to an electromagnetic field is invariant under the joint gauge transformation [231]
\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},t)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle e^{iq\Lambda(\mathbf{x},t)}\;\psi(\mathbf{x},t)
(B.46)
\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},t)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x},t)+\nabla\Lambda(\mathbf{x},t)
(B.47)
\displaystyle{V}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},t)
\displaystyle=
\displaystyle V(\mathbf{x},t)-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Lambda(\mathbf{x},t)\;.
(B.48)
The canonical momentum \hat{\mathbf{P}}=-i\nabla is not gauge invariant, but transforms in the Heisenberg picture as
\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{\prime}(t)=\hat{\mathbf{P}}(t)+q\nabla\Lambda\left(\hat{\mathbf{X}}(t),t\right)\;,
(B.49)
and therefore Alice cannot measure it directly. The reader can easily check using (B.47) and (B.49) that the velocity operator given in the main text is gauge invariant, as it has to be. Alice can then measure directly the velocity, and reconstruct from it the canonical momentum. However, the relation between them
\hat{\mathbf{P}}=m\;\hat{\mathbf{V}}+q\;\hat{\mathbf{A}}\left(\hat{\mathbf{X}}\right)
(B.50)
contains the vector potential, that from (B.47) is not gauge invariant, and cannot be directly measured. In the Coulomb gauge, it is possible to invert (B.9) and express the vector potential in terms of the magnetic field, that is gauge invariant and can be actually measured by Alice:
\hat{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\frac{\nabla\times\hat{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{y})}{\left|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\right|}\;d^{3}y\;.
(B.51)
Putting together (B.51) and (B.50), we get
\hat{\mathbf{P}}=m\;\hat{\mathbf{V}}+\frac{q}{4\pi}\int\frac{\nabla\times\hat{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{y})}{\left|\hat{\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{y}\right|}\;d^{3}y\;.
(B.52)
However, reconstructing the canonical momentum from the velocity with (B.52) requires Alice to measure the magnetic field in the whole space. Even if she can allow for some error in the reconstruction, the region in which she has to measure the field increases with the charge q , and can extend well outside the support of the wavefunction.
B.8 Detector switching time
The switching time could be due to many specific technical difficulties but, in principle, the only unavoidable limitation is imposed by relativistic causality. If the linear size of the trap is L , then Bob cannot remove the trap instantaneously since he needs at least a time L/c to induce any change in the apparatus. Moreover the size of the trap cannot be arbitrarily small but should be at least larger than the position uncertainty of the test mass, i.e. L\geq\Delta X . Thus, a rough estimate of the minimum switching time is given up to numerical factors by
T_{D}\simeq\frac{\Delta X}{c}.
(B.53)
If we want to take into account also this finite response time of the experimental apparatus, the causality equation
(10.2) should be changed into
T_{A}+T_{B}+T_{D}\geq\frac{R}{c},
(B.54)
where T_{A} is Alice measurement time and T_{B} is the entanglement generation time. We remind that the lower bounds for the measurement time that we obtained (for T_{D}=0 ) are:
T_{A}\geq\frac{q\,d}{q_{\rm P}c}\;,\quad q>q_{\rm P}\qquad\left|\qquad T_{A}\geq\frac{m\,d}{m_{\rm P}c}\;,\quad m>m_{\rm P}\;,\right.
(B.55)
as given by Eq.s (10.3) and (10.5) and corresponding to a quantum superposition of a charge q and mass m respectively. For a finite switching time T_{D} , from
(B.53) and (B.54) we get the weaker bounds
T_{A}\geq\frac{q\,d}{q_{\rm P}c}-\frac{\Delta X}{c}\;,\quad q>q_{\rm P}\;,\qquad\left|\qquad T_{A}\geq\frac{m\,d}{m_{\rm P}c}-\frac{\Delta X}{c}\;,\quad m>m_{\rm P}\;.\right.
(B.56)
However we remind that in the derivation of the optimal detection experiment we deduced that the trap should be as narrow as possible in order to minimize \Delta X (ideally down to the charge radius or to the Planck length). In this regime we always have d\gg\Delta X and the correction terms appearing in Eq.s
(B.56) are negligible.

Instructions for reporting errors
We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
methods listed below:
Click the "Report Issue" (
) button, located in the page header.
Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
all.
Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion, and welcome developer contributions.
We gratefully acknowledge support from
our major funders,
member institutions, ,
and all contributors.
